Optimal Acceleration for Minimax and Fixed-Point Problems is Not Unique
TaeHo Yoon, Jaeyeon Kim, Jaewook J. Suh, Ernest K. Ryu

Abstract
Recently, accelerated algorithms using the anchoring mechanism for minimax optimization and fixed-point problems have been proposed, and matching complexity lower bounds establish their optimality. In this work, we present the surprising observation that the optimal acceleration mechanism in minimax optimization and fixed-point problems is not unique. Our new algorithms achieve exactly the same worst-case convergence rates as existing anchor-based methods while using materially different acceleration mechanisms. Specifically, these new algorithms are dual to the prior anchor-based accelerated methods in the sense of H-duality. This finding opens a new avenue of research on accelerated algorithms since we now have a family of methods that empirically exhibit varied characteristics while having the same optimal worst-case guarantee.



1
Introduction

Accelerated algorithms using the so-called anchoring mechanism have been recently proposed for solving minimax optimization and fixed-point problems. Furthermore, these algorithms are optimal: for minimax problems, the gap between the upper and lower bounds is a constant factor of
16
, and for fixed-point problems, there is no gap, not even a constant factor. Therefore, anchoring was thought to be “the” correct acceleration mechanism for these setups.

In this work, however, we present the surprising observation that the optimal acceleration mechanism in minimax optimization and fixed-point problems is not unique. For minimax optimization, we introduce a new algorithm with the same worst-case rate as the best-known algorithm. For fixed-point problems, we introduce a continuous family of exact optimal algorithms, all achieving the same worst-case rate that exactly matches the known lower bound.
The representative cases of our new accelerated algorithms are dual algorithms of the prior anchor-based accelerated algorithms in the sense of H-duality. The resulting new acceleration mechanisms are materially different from the existing anchoring mechanism.

These findings show that anchor-based acceleration is not unique and sufficient as the mechanism of achieving the exact optimal complexity and enable us to correctly reframe the study of optimal acceleration as a study of a
family
of acceleration mechanisms rather than a singular one. This shift in perspective will likely be critical in the future research toward a more complete and fundamental understanding of accelerations in fixed-point and minimax problems.

1.1
Preliminaries

We use standard notation for set-valued operators
(
Bauschke & Combettes, 2017
;
Ryu & Yin, 2022
)
.
An operator
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a set-valued function (so
{\mathbb{A}}(x)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
).
For simplicity, we write
{\mathbb{A}}x={\mathbb{A}}(x)
.
The graph of
{\mathbb{A}}
is defined and denoted as
\mathrm{Gra}\,{\mathbb{A}}=\{(x,y)\,|\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},y\in{\mathbb{A}}x\}
.
The inverse of
{\mathbb{A}}
is defined by
{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}y=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,y\in{\mathbb{A}}x\}
.
Scalar multiples and sums of operators are defined in the Minkowski sense.
If
{\mathbb{T}}x
is a singleton for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we write
{\mathbb{T}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and treat it as a function.
An operator
{\mathbb{T}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
L
-Lipschitz (
L>0
) if
\left\|{\mathbb{T}}x-{\mathbb{T}}y\right\|\leq L\left\|x-y\right\|
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We say
{\mathbb{T}}
is nonexpansive if it is
1
-Lipschitz.

A function
{\mathbf{L}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
is convex-concave if
{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)
is convex in
u
for all fixed
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and concave in
v
for all fixed
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
If
{\mathbf{L}}(u_{\star},v)\leq{\mathbf{L}}(u_{\star},v_{\star})\leq{\mathbf{L}}(u,v_{\star})
for all
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then
(u_{\star},v_{\star})
is a saddle point of
{\mathbf{L}}
.
For
L>0
, if
{\mathbf{L}}
is differentiable and
\nabla{\mathbf{L}}
is
L
-Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we say
{\mathbf{L}}
is
L
-smooth.
In this case, we define the saddle operator of
{\mathbf{L}}
by
\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(u,v)=\left(\nabla_{u}{\mathbf{L}}(u,v),-\nabla_{v}{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)\right)
.
In most of the cases, we use the joint variable notation
x=(u,v)
and concisely write
\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x)
in place of
\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(u,v)
.

1.2
Related work

Here, we quickly review the most closely related prior work while deferring the more comprehensive literature survey to
Appendix

A
.

Fixed-point algorithms.

A fixed-point problem solves

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}\underset{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\text{find}}&y={\mathbb{T}}y\end{array}

for
{\mathbb{T}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The magnitude of the fixed-point residual
y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}
is one natural performance measure.
Sabach & Shtern (2017)
first achieved the rate
\left\|y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1/k^{2})
through the Sequential Averaging Method, and

Lieder (2021)
showed that Halpern iteration with specific parameters, which we call
OHM
in
Section

2.1
, improves upon the rate of
Sabach & Shtern (2017)
by a factor of
16
.
Furthermore,
Park & Ryu (2022)
provided a matching complexity lower bound showing that the rate of
Lieder (2021)
is exactly optimal.

Minimax algorithms.

Minimax optimization solves

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}\underset{u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\text{minimize}}\,\,\underset{v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}{\text{maximize}}&{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)\end{array}

for
{\mathbf{L}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Under the assumption of convex-concavity,
\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(u_{k},v_{k})\right\|^{2}
is one natural performance measure.

Yoon & Ryu (2021)
first provided the (order-optimal) accelerated
\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(u_{k},v_{k})\right\|^{2}={\mathcal{O}}(1/k^{2})
rate via the Extra Anchored Gradient (EAG) algorithm together with
\Omega(1/k^{2})
complexity lower bound. The Fast Extragradient (FEG) algorithm of
(
Lee & Kim, 2021
)
then improved this rate by a constant factor, achieving the currently best-known constant.

Duality of algorithms.

H-duality
(
Kim et al., 2023a
;
Kim et al., 2023b
)
is a duality correspondence between algorithms.
The H-duality theory of
Kim et al. (2023a)
shows that in smooth convex minimization, an algorithm’s rate with respect to function value translates to the rate of its H-dual algorithm with respect to gradient norm and vice versa.
Our paper establishes a different H-duality theory for fixed-point algorithms.

1.3
Contribution and organization

Contributions.

This work is presenting a new class of accelerations in fixed-point and minimax problems. Our findings provide the perspective that the study of optimal acceleration must be viewed as a study of a
family
of acceleration mechanisms rather than a singular one.

Organization.

Section

2
provides an overview of the novel algorithms
Dual-OHM
and
Dual-FEG
and their continuous-time model.

Section

3
presents the analysis of
Dual-OHM
.

Section

4
presents an infinite family of fixed-point algorithms achieving the same exact optimal rates.

Section

5
presents the H-duality for fixed-point problems, which explains the symmetry and connection underlying
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
.

Section

6
provides the analysis of
Dual-FEG
and its H-dual relationship with
FEG
.

Section

7
explores a continuous-time perspective of the new algorithms.

Section

8
provides numerical simulations.

2
Summary of new acceleration results

In this section, we provide an overview of novel accelerated algorithms for several setups.
For each setup, we first review the existing (primal) algorithm using the anchor acceleration mechanism and then show its dual counterpart with identical rates but using a materially different acceleration mechanism. (The meaning “dual” is clarified later.)
Throughout the paper, we write
N\geq 1
to denote the pre-specified iteration count of the algorithm.

2.1
Fixed-point problems

Consider the fixed-point problem (
1.2
), where
{\mathbb{T}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is nonexpansive.
We denote
\mathrm{Fix}\,{\mathbb{T}}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,y={\mathbb{T}}y\}
and assume
\mathrm{Fix}\,{\mathbb{T}}\neq\emptyset
.

The (primal) Optimal Halpern Method (
OHM
)
1
1

1

Some prior work referred to this method as the “Optimized” Halpern Method, but we now know the method is (exactly) optimal as
(
Park & Ryu, 2022
)
provided a matching lower bound.

(
Halpern, 1967
;
Lieder, 2021
)
is

\displaystyle y_{k+1}=\frac{k+1}{k+2}{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}+\frac{1}{k+2}y_{0}

(OHM)

for
k=0,1,\dots
. Equivalently, we can write

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\frac{1}{k+2}(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k})+\frac{k}{k+2}\left({\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}\right)

where we define
{\mathbb{T}}y_{-1}=y_{0}
.

OHM
exhibits the rate

\displaystyle\|y_{k-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{k^{2}}

for
k=1,2,\dots
and
y_{\star}\in\mathrm{Fix}\,{\mathbb{T}}

(
Lieder, 2021
)
.

We present the new method,
Dual Optimal Halpern Method (
Dual-OHM
):

\displaystyle y_{k+1}=y_{k}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left({\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}\right)

(Dual-OHM)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
, where we define
{\mathbb{T}}y_{-1}=y_{0}
.
Equivalently,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} z_{k+1}&=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-\frac{1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right)\\
y_{k+1}&={\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-z_{k+1}\end{aligned}

(3)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
, where
z_{0}=0
.

Dual-OHM
exhibits the rate

\displaystyle\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{N^{2}}

for
y_{\star}\in\mathrm{Fix}\,{\mathbb{T}}
.
This rate exactly coincides with the rate of

OHM
for
k=N
.
We discuss the detailed analysis in
Section

3
.

As shown in
(
Park & Ryu, 2022
)
, there exists a nonexpansive operator
{\mathbb{T}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 2N-2
such that

\displaystyle\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}\geq\frac{4\left\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{N^{2}}

for any deterministic algorithm using
N-1
evaluations of
{\mathbb{T}}
.
Therefore,
OHM
is exactly optimal; it cannot be improved, not even by a constant factor, in terms of worst-case performance.
The discovery of
Dual-OHM
is surprising as it shows that the exact optimal algorithm is not unique.

2.2
Smooth convex-concave minimax optimization

Consider the minimax optimization problem (
1.2
),
where
{\mathbf{L}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
is convex-concave and
L
-smooth.
Convex-concave minimax problems are closely related to fixed-point problems, and the anchoring mechanism of
OHM
for accelerating fixed-point algorithms has been used to accelerate algorithms for minimax problems
(
Yoon & Ryu, 2021
;
Lee & Kim, 2021
)
.
We show that
Dual-OHM
also has its minimax counterpart.
In the following, write
{\mathbb{A}}=\nabla_{\pm}{\mathbf{L}}
for notational conciseness.

The (primal) Fast Extragradient
2
2

2

FEG
was designed primarily for weakly nonconvex-nonconcave problems, but we consider its application to the special case of convex-concave problems.
(
FEG
)
(
Lee & Kim, 2021
)
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}x_{k+1/2}&=x_{k}+\frac{1}{k+1}(x_{0}-x_{k})-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\\
x_{k+1}&=x_{k}+\frac{1}{k+1}(x_{0}-x_{k})-\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}\end{split}

(FEG)

for
k=0,1,\dots
.
If
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{L}
,
FEG
exhibits the rate

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(x_{k})\right\|^{2}=\left\|{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|x_{0}-x_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}k^{2}}

for
k=1,2,\dots
and a saddle point (solution)
x_{\star}=(u_{\star},v_{\star})
.
To the best of our knowledge, this result with
\alpha=\frac{1}{L}
is the fastest known rate.

We present the new method, Dual Fast Extragradient (
Dual-FEG
):

\displaystyle x_{k+1/2}=x_{k}-\alpha z_{k}-\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}

\displaystyle x_{k+1}=x_{k+1/2}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha\left({\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right)

\displaystyle z_{k+1}=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-\frac{1}{N-k}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}

(Dual-FEG)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-1
, where
z_{0}=0
.
For
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{L}
,
Dual-FEG
exhibits the rate

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(x_{N})\right\|^{2}=\left\|{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|x_{0}-x_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}N^{2}}.

This rate exactly coincides with the rate of
FEG
for
k=N
.
We discuss the detailed analysis in Section
6
.

As shown in
(
Yoon & Ryu, 2021
)
, there exists
L
-smooth convex-concave
{\mathbf{L}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
m=n\geq 3N+2
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(x_{N})\right\|^{2}\geq\frac{L^{2}\left\|x_{0}-x_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{\left(2\lfloor N/2\rfloor+1\right)^{2}}

for any deterministic
N
-step first-order algorithm.
Therefore,
FEG
and
Dual-FEG
are optimal up to a constant factor.

2.3
Continuous-time analysis

This section introduces continuous-time analyses corresponding to the algorithms of Sections
2.1
and
2.2
.
In continuous-time limits, the algorithms for fixed-point problems and minimax optimization reduce to the same continuous-time ODE.
Let
{\mathbb{A}}=2({\mathbb{I}}+{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}-{\mathbb{I}}
for fixed-point problem (
1.2
) and
{\mathbb{A}}=\nabla_{\pm}{\mathbf{L}}
for minimax problem (
1.2
).

The (primal) Anchor ODE
(
Ryu et al., 2019
;
Suh et al., 2023
)
is

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))+\frac{1}{t}(X_{0}-X(t))

(4)

which has an equivalent 2nd-order form

\ddot{X}(t)+\frac{2}{t}\dot{X}+\frac{1}{t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))+\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=0

where
X(0)=X_{0}
(and
\dot{X}(0)=-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})
for 2nd-order form) is the initial condition.
Anchor ODE exhibits the rate

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|X_{0}-X_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{t^{2}}

for
t>0
, where
X_{\star}
is a solution (zero of
{\mathbb{A}}
).

We present the new Dual-Anchor ODE:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dot{X}(t)&=-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\\
\dot{Z}(t)&=-\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)),\end{aligned}

(5)

which has an equivalent 2nd-order form

\displaystyle\ddot{X}(t)+\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)+\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=0

for
t\in(0,T)
, where
T>0
is a pre-specified terminal time, and
X(0)=X_{0}
and
Z(0)=0
(or
\dot{X}(0)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})
for the 2nd-order form) are initial conditions.
Dual-Anchor ODE exhibits the rate

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|X_{0}-X_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{T^{2}}.

This rate exactly coincides with the rate of Anchor ODE for
t=T
.
We discuss the detailed analysis in Section
7
.

“Dual” in the sense of H-duality.

Our new algorithms are “dual” to the known primal algorithms in the sense of H-duality, a recently developed notion of duality between convex minimization algorithms
(
Kim et al., 2023a
)
. We provide the detailed discussion of H-duality for fixed-point algorithms in
Section

5
.

3
Analysis of
Dual-OHM

In this section, we present the convergence analysis of
Dual-OHM
, showing that it is another exact optimal algorithm for solving nonexpansive fixed-point problems.

3.0
Preliminaries: Monotone operators

In the following, we express our analysis using the language of monotone operators. We quickly set up the notation and review the connections between fixed-point problems and monotone operators.

Monotone operators.

A set-valued (non-linear) operator
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
is monotone if
\left\langle g-g^{\prime},x-x^{\prime}\right\rangle\geq 0
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
g\in{\mathbb{A}}x
, and
g^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{A}}x^{\prime}
.
If
{\mathbb{A}}
is monotone and there is no monotone operator
{\mathbb{A}}^{\prime}
for which
\mathrm{Gra}\,{\mathbb{A}}\subset\mathrm{Gra}\,{\mathbb{A}}^{\prime}
properly, then
{\mathbb{A}}
is maximally monotone.
If
{\mathbb{A}}
is maximally monotone, then its resolvent
{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}:=({\mathbb{I}}+{\mathbb{A}})^{-1}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a well-defined single-valued operator.

Fixed-point problems are monotone inclusion problems.

There exists a natural correspondence between the classes of nonexpansive operators and maximally monotone operators in the following sense.

Proposition 3.1
.

(
Eckstein & Bertsekas, 1992
, Theorem 2)

If
{\mathbb{T}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a nonexpansive operator, then
{\mathbb{A}}=2({\mathbb{I}}+{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}-{\mathbb{I}}
is maximally monotone.
Conversely, if
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
is maximally monotone, then
{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
is nonexpansive.

When
{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
, we have
x={\mathbb{T}}x\iff 0\in{\mathbb{A}}x
,
i.e.,
\mathrm{Fix}\,{\mathbb{T}}=\mathrm{Zer}\,{\mathbb{A}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,0\in{\mathbb{A}}x\}
.
Therefore,
Proposition

3.1
induces a one-to-one correspondence between nonexpansive fixed-point problems and monotone inclusion problems.

Fixed-point residual norm and operator norm.

Given
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, its accuracy as an approximate fixed-point solution is often measured by
\|y-{\mathbb{T}}y\|
, the norm of fixed-point residual.
Let
{\mathbb{A}}
be the maximal monotone operator satisfying
{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
, and let
x={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}y
.
Then we see that

\displaystyle y\in({\mathbb{I}}+{\mathbb{A}})(x)=x+{\mathbb{A}}x\iff y-x\in{\mathbb{A}}x.

Denote
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x=y-x\in{\mathbb{A}}x
.
Then

\displaystyle y-{\mathbb{T}}y=y-(2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}})(y)=2(y-{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}y)=2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x.

Therefore,
\|y-{\mathbb{T}}y\|=2\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x\|
.

Minimax optimization and monotone operators.

The minimax problem (
1.2
) can also be recast as a monotone inclusion problem.
Precisely, for
L
-smooth convex-concave
{\mathbf{L}}
, its saddle operator
\nabla_{\pm}{\mathbf{L}}
is monotone and
L
-Lipschitz.
In this case,
x_{\star}=(u_{\star},v_{\star})
is a minimax solution for
{\mathbf{L}}
if and only if
\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{\star})=0
.

Finally, we quickly state a handy lemma used in the convergence analyses throughout the paper.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
be monotone and let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x\in{\mathbb{A}}x
.
Suppose, for some
\rho>0
,

\displaystyle\rho\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x\right\|^{2}+\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x,x-y\right\rangle\leq 0

holds.
Then, for
x_{\star}\in\mathrm{Zer}\,{\mathbb{A}}
,
\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{\left\|y-x_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{\rho^{2}}
.

Proof.

By monotonicity of
{\mathbb{A}}
and Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\rho\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x\right\|^{2}+\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x,x-y\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\rho\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x\right\|^{2}+\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x,x_{\star}-y\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\rho}{2}\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x\right\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2\rho}\left\|x_{\star}-y\right\|^{2}.\vskip-12.0pt\qed

3.1
Convergence analyses of
OHM
and
Dual-OHM

We formally state the convergence result of
Dual-OHM
and outline its proof.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
{\mathbb{T}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonexpansive and
y_{\star}\in\mathrm{Fix}\,{\mathbb{T}}
.
For
N\geq 1
,
Dual-OHM
exhibits the rate

\displaystyle\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\|^{2}}{N^{2}}.

Proof outline.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}
be the unique maximally monotone operator such that
{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
(defined as in
Proposition

3.1
).
Let
x_{k+1}={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}(y_{k})
for
k=0,1,\dots
, so that
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}=y_{k}-x_{k+1}\in{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}
.
Recall the alternative form (
3
) of
Dual-OHM
.
Define

\displaystyle V_{k}

\displaystyle=-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left\|z_{k}+2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle z_{k}+2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N},y_{k}-y_{N-1}\right\rangle

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-1
.
We show in
Appendix

C
that

\displaystyle V_{k}-V_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\langle x_{N}-x_{k+1},\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}\right\rangle,

i.e.,
V_{k}\geq V_{k+1}
for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
.
Observe that
V_{N-1}=0
and because
z_{0}=0
,

\displaystyle V_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4(N-1)}{N}\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{4}{N}\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N},y_{0}-y_{N-1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-4\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{4}{N}\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N},y_{0}-x_{N}\right\rangle

where the second line uses
x_{N}=y_{N-1}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}
.
Finally, divide both sides of
V_{0}\geq\cdots\geq V_{N-1}=0
by
\frac{4}{N}
, apply
Lemma

3.2
and the identity
y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}=2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}
:

\displaystyle\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}=4\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\|^{2}}{N^{2}}.

∎

We point out that the convergence analysis for
OHM
can be done in a similar style
(
Lieder, 2021
)
.
Define
{\mathbb{A}}
,
x_{k+1}
and
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}
as above.
Define
U_{0}=0
and

\displaystyle U_{k}=k^{2}\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}\right\|^{2}+k\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k},x_{k}-y_{0}\right\rangle

for
k=1,2,\dots
.
It can be shown that
(
Ryu & Yin, 2022
)

\displaystyle U_{j}-U_{j+1}=j(j+1)\left\langle x_{j+1}-x_{j},\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}{x_{j+1}}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}{x_{j}}\right\rangle\geq 0

for
j=0,1,\dots
.
Then
0=U_{0}\geq\cdots\geq U_{k}
, and dividing both sides by
k
and applying
Lemma

3.2
gives the rate
\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\left\|y_{0}-x_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{k^{2}}
.

3.2
Proximal forms of
OHM
and
Dual-OHM

It is known that
OHM
can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}(y_{k})

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=x_{k+1}+\frac{k}{k+2}(x_{k+1}-x_{k})-\frac{k}{k+2}(x_{k}-y_{k-1})

for
k=0,1,\dots
, where
x_{0}=y_{0}
. This proximal form is called Accelerated Proximal Point Method (APPM)
(
Kim, 2021
)
.
Likewise,
Dual-OHM
can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}(y_{k})

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=x_{k+1}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(x_{k+1}-x_{k})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(x_{k}-y_{k-1})-\frac{1}{N-k}(x_{k+1}-y_{k})

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
, where
x_{-1}=y_{0}=x_{0}
.
We prove the equivalence in
Appendix

B
.

4
Continuous family of exact optimal fixed-point algorithms

Upon seeing the two algorithms
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
exhibiting the same exact optimal rate, it is natural to ask whether there are other exact optimal algorithms. In this section, we show that there is, in fact, an
(N-2)
-dimensional continuous family of exact optimal algorithms.

4.1
H-matrix representation

Fixed-point algorithm of
N-1
iterations with fixed (non-adaptive) step-sizes can be written in the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}y_{k+1}&=y_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}h_{k+1,j+1}\underbrace{(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})}_{2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}}\end{split}

(6)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
, where the
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}
notation uses the convention of Section
3
.
With this representation, the lower-triangular matrix
H\in\mathbb{R}^{(N-1)\times(N-1)}
, defined by
(H)_{k,j}=h_{k,j}
if
j\leq k
and
(H)_{k,j}=0
otherwise, fully specifies the algorithm.

4.2
Optimal algorithm family via H-matrices

We now state our result characterizing a family of exact optimal algorithms.

Theorem 4.1

(Optimal family)
.

There exist a nonempty open convex set
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{N-2}
and a continuous injective mapping
\Phi\colon C\to\mathbb{R}^{(N-1)\times(N-1)}
such that for all
w\in C

H=\Phi(w)
is lower-triangular, and algorithm (
6
) defined via the H-matrix
H=\Phi(w)
exhibits the exact optimal rate (matching the lower bound)

\displaystyle\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}=4\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{N^{2}}

for nonexpansive
{\mathbb{T}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
y_{\star}\in\mathrm{Fix}\,{\mathbb{T}}
.

We briefly outline the high-level idea of the proof while deferring the details to Appendix
E
.
From
Section

3.1
, we observe that the convergence proofs for
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
both work by establishing the identity

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&=\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N},x_{N}-y_{0}\right\rangle+N\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}\|^{2}\\
&\quad\quad+\sum_{(i,j)\in I}\lambda_{i,j}\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{i}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j},x_{i}-x_{j}\right\rangle,\end{aligned}

(7)

where
I
is some set of tuple of indices
(i,j)
with
i>j

(for both algorithms, the consecutive differences of Lyapunov functions are of the form
\lambda_{i,j}\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{i}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j},x_{i}-x_{j}\right\rangle
; sum them up to obtain (
7
)) and
\lambda_{i,j}\geq 0
.
For
OHM
,
I
consists of
(k+1,k)
for
k=1,\dots,N-1
while for
Dual-OHM
,
(N,k)
for
k=1,\dots,N-1
are used.
In the proof of
Theorem

4.1
, we identify algorithms (in terms of H-matrices) whose convergence can be proved via (
7
) where
I
is the union of the two sets of tuples, i.e.,

\displaystyle I=\{(k+1,k)\,|\,k=1,\dots,N-1\}

\displaystyle\cup\,\{(N,k)\,|\,k=1,\dots,N-1\}

.

Once (
7
) is established, we obtain the desired convergence rate from monotonicity of
{\mathbb{A}}
and
Lemma

3.2
.

To clarify, the algorithm family as defined in
Theorem

4.1
does not include
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
as
C
is an open set and
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
correspond to points on the boundary
\partial C=\overline{C}\backslash C
.
We choose not to incorporate
\partial C
in
Theorem

4.1
because doing so leads to cumbersome divisions-by-zero. However, with a specialized analysis,
Proposition

4.2
shows that
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
are indeed a part of the parametrization
\Phi

and therefore that the optimal family “connects”
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
.
The proof is deferred to
Section

F.1
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Consider
C
and
\Phi
defined in Theorem
4.1
.
One can continuously extend
\Phi
to some
u,v\in\partial C
such that
\Phi(u)=H_{\text{OHM}}
and
\Phi(v)=H_{\text{Dual-OHM}}
, where
H_{\text{OHM}}
and
H_{\text{Dual-OHM}}
are respectively the H-matrix representations of
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
.

Our proof of
Theorem

4.1
comes without an explicit characterization of
\Phi
or the H-matrices (we only show existence
3
3

3

The H-matrices are, however, “computable” in the sense that they can be obtained by solving an explicit system of linear equations, as outlined in
Appendix

E
.
).
Fortunately, for
N=3
, we do have a simple explicit characterization, which directly illustrates the family interpolating between the
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
.
For any
2\times 2
lower-triangular
\begin{bmatrix}h_{1,1}&0\\
h_{2,1}&h_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}
satisfying
h_{2,1}=1-h_{1,1}-h_{2,2}
,
h_{1,1}h_{2,2}=\frac{1}{3}
and
h_{1,1},h_{2,2}\in\left[\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3}\right]
, the iterate
y_{2}
defined by (
6
) satisfies
\left\|y_{2}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{2}\right\|^{2}\hfill=4\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{3}\right\|^{2}\hfill\leq\hfill\frac{4\left\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{9}
.
In particular,
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
each correspond to
(h_{1,1},h_{2,2})=\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3}\right)
and
(h_{1,1},h_{2,2})=\left(\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{2}\right)
.

As a final comment, we clarify that
Theorem

4.1
is not the exhaustive parametrization of optimal algorithms; there are other exact optimal algorithms that
Theorem

4.1
does not cover.
We provide such examples in Appendix
F.3
.
The complete characterization of the set of exact optimal algorithms seems to be challenging, and we leave it to future work.

5
H-duality for fixed-point algorithms

In this section, we present an H-duality theory for fixed-point algorithms.
At a high level, H-duality states that an algorithm and its convergence proof can be
dualized
in a certain sense.
This result provides a formal connection between
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
.

5.1
H-dual operation

Before providing the formal definition, we observe the following.
The H-matrix of
OHM
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(H_{\text{OHM}}\right)_{k,j}=\begin{cases}-\frac{j}{k(k+1)}&\text{if }j<k\\
\frac{k}{k+1}&\text{if }j=k\end{cases}\end{split}

while the H-matrix of
Dual-OHM
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(H_{\text{Dual-OHM}}\right)_{k,j}=\begin{cases}-\frac{N-k}{(N-j)(N-j+1)}&\text{if }j<k\\
\frac{N-k}{N-k+1}&\text{if }j=k.\end{cases}\end{split}

We derive the H-matrix representations in
Appendix

B
.
For now, note the relationship
H_{\text{Dual-OHM}}=H_{\text{OHM}}^{\text{A}}
, where superscript
A
denotes anti-diagonal transpose
\left(H^{\text{A}}\right)_{k,j}=\left(H\right)_{N-j,N-k}
.
The anti-diagonal transpose operation reflects a square matrix along its anti-diagonal direction and preserves lower triangularity.

Given an algorithm represented by
H
, we define its
H-dual
as the algorithm represented by
H^{\text{A}}
.
In other words, given a general iterative algorithm defined as (
6
), its H-dual is the algorithm with the update rule

\displaystyle\begin{split}y_{k+1}&=y_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}h_{N-j-1,N-k-1}\left(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j}\right)\end{split}

(8)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Dual-OHM
and
OHM
are H-duals of each other.

5.2
H-duality theorem

OHM
and
Dual-OHM
are H-duals of each other, and they share the identical rates on
\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}
.
This symmetry is not a coincidence; the following H-duality theorem explains the connection between them.

Theorem 5.2

(Informal)
.

Let
H\in\mathbb{R}^{(N-1)\times(N-1)}
be lower triangular and
\nu>0
. Then the following are equivalent.

•

\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}\leq\nu\left\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\right\|^{2}
for (
6
) with
H
can be proved with primal Lyapunov structure.

•

\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}\leq\nu\left\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\right\|^{2}
for (
6
) with
H^{\text{A}}
(H-dual) can be proved with dual Lyapunov structure.

We defer the precise statement and the proof of
Theorem

5.2
to
Appendix

D
. The high-level takeaway of
Theorem

5.2
is as follows: If an algorithm achieves a certain convergence rate with respect to
\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}
(based on a certain proof structure), then the proof can be
H-dualized
to guarantee the exact same convergence rate for its H-dual. The relationship between
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
is an instance of this duality correspondence (with
\nu=1/N^{2}
).

Different from the prior H-duality result for convex minimization
(
Kim et al., 2023a
)
, we show that algorithms in H-dual relationship share the convergence rate with respect to the same performance measure
\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}
and the same initial condition
\left\|y_{0}-y_{\star}\right\|^{2}
. One could say that the performance measure
\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}
is “self-dual”. This contrasts with the prior H-duality
(
Kim et al., 2023a
)
, which showed that function values are “dual” to gradient magnitude in convex minimization. While the H-duality theory of this work and that of
(
Kim et al., 2023a
)
share some superficial similarities, the unifying fundamental principle remains unknown, and finding one is an interesting subject of future research.

6
Analysis of
Dual-FEG
for minimax problems​

(a)

Trajectories from a 2D bilinear example

(b)

Trajectories from a 2D non-bilinear example

Figure 1
:
Trajectories generated by minimax optimization algorithms on
(left)

{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)=uv
with a random initial point of norm 1 and
(right)

{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)=u^{2}v
with initial point
(-1,1)
, where the dashed vertical line indicates the set of optima (saddle points).

In this section, we present the convergence analysis of
Dual-FEG
and additionally provide the observation that
Dual-FEG
is the H-dual of
FEG
.

Algorithms such as
OHM
and
Dual-OHM
are sometimes referred to as “implicit methods” since they can be expressed using resolvents, as discussed in Section
3.2
. The results of this section show that
Dual-OHM
has an “explicit” counterpart
Dual-FEG
, which uses direct gradient evaluations instead.

First, we formally state the convergence result.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{L}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
be convex-concave and
L
-smooth.
For
N\geq 1
fixed, if
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{L}
,
Dual-FEG
exhibits the rate

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(x_{N})\right\|^{2}=\left\|\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{N})\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|x_{0}-x_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}N^{2}},

where
x_{\star}\in\mathrm{Zer}\,\nabla_{\pm}{\mathbf{L}}
is a solution.

Proof outline.

Define
g_{N}=\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{N})
.
In
Section

G.1
, we show that

\displaystyle V_{k}=-\alpha\left\|z_{k}+g_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle z_{k}+g_{N},x_{k}-x_{N}\right\rangle

is nonincreasing in
k=0,1,\dots,N-1
, and
V_{N-1}\geq 0
.
This implies
0\leq V_{0}=-\alpha\left\|g_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{N}\left\langle g_{N},x_{0}-x_{N}\right\rangle
.
Finally, divide both sides by
\frac{2}{N}
and apply
Lemma

3.2
.
∎

Dual-FEG
and
FEG
are H-duals.

Dual-FEG
has intermediate iterates
x_{k+1/2}
, serving a role similar to intermediate iterates of
FEG
.
Consider the following H-matrix representation for
Dual-FEG
, analogous to (
6
):

\displaystyle x_{(\ell+1)/2}=x_{\ell/2}-\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}h_{(\ell+1)/2,i/2}\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{i/2})

for
\ell=0,1,\dots,2N-1
.
For
Dual-FEG
, the H-matrix is the
2N\times 2N
lower-triangular matrix

\displaystyle(H_{\text{Dual-FEG}})_{\ell,i}=\begin{cases}h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}&\text{if }i\leq\ell\\
0&\text{if }i>\ell.\end{cases}

We can analogously define the H-matrix
H_{\text{FEG}}
for
FEG
, which also has extragradient-type intermediate iterates.
It turns out that
H_{\text{FEG}}
and
H_{\text{Dual-FEG}}
are anti-diagonal transposes of each other.

Proposition 6.2
.

Dual-FEG
and
FEG
are H-duals of each other.

We defer the proof to
Section

G.2
.
This intriguing H-dual relationship and identical convergence rates of
FEG
and
Dual-FEG
strongly indicate the possible existence of H-duality theory for smooth minimax optimization; we leave its formal treatment to future work.

7
Continuous-time analysis of dual-anchoring

(a)

Worst case bilinear problem

(b)

Strongly-convex-strongly-concave bilinear problem

Figure 2
:
Performance of minimax algorithms in reducing
\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(x_{k})\right\|^{2}
for bilinear problem instances. The dashed black line indicates the theoretical upper bounds for
FEG
.

In this section, we outline the analysis of the Dual-Anchor ODE (
5
), which is the common continuous-time model for both
Dual-OHM
and
Dual-FEG
, as derived in
Section

H.1
.
We then introduce the notion of H-dual for ODE models and show that Dual-Anchor ODE is the H-dual of Anchor ODE (
4
), the continuous-time model for
OHM
and
FEG
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be Lipschitz continuous and monotone.
For
T>0
, the solution
X\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the Dual-Anchor ODE (
5
) with initial conditions
X(0)=X_{0},Z(0)=0
uniquely exists, and
X(T)=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}X(t)
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|X_{0}-X_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{T^{2}}

where
X_{\star}\in\mathrm{Zer}\,{\mathbb{A}}
.

Proof outline.

Define
V\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle V(t)

\displaystyle=-\left\|Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{2}{T-t}\left\langle Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),X(t)-X(T)\right\rangle.

In
Section

H.3.3
, we show that

\displaystyle\dot{V}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\left\langle X(t)-X(T),{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\rangle}{(T-t)^{2}}\leq 0.

Corollary

H.4
shows
\lim_{t\to T^{-}}V(t)=0
.
From
Z(0)=0
,

\displaystyle V(0)=-\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}-\frac{2}{T}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),X(T)-X_{0}\right\rangle.

Dividing both sides of
0=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}V(t)\leq V(0)
by
\frac{2}{T}
and applying
Lemma

3.2
we get the desired inequality.
∎

Dual-Anchor ODE and Anchor ODE are H-duals.

The continuous-time analogue of the H-matrix representation
(
Kim & Yang, 2023b
;
Kim et al., 2023a
)
is

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}H(t,s){\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds,

where we refer to
H(t,s)
as the
H-kernel
. The
H-dual ODE
is defined by

\dot{X}(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}H^{\text{A}}(t,s){\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds

where
H^{\text{A}}(t,s)=H(T-s,T-t)
is the analogue of the anti-diagonal transpose.
We prove the following in
Section

H.2
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Dual-Anchor ODE (
5
) and Anchor ODE (
4
) are H-duals of each other.

Generalization to differential inclusion.

Theorem

7.1
assumes Lipschitz continuity of
{\mathbb{A}}
for simplicity.
However, even if we only assume that
{\mathbb{A}}
is maximally monotone, the existence of a solution and the convergence result can be established for the differential inclusion

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)

\displaystyle\in-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(t)

\displaystyle\in-\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)),

which is a generalized continuous-time model for possibly set-valued operators.
We provide the details in
Section

H.4
.

8
Experiments

In this section, we present some numerical simulations illustrating the dynamics of dual-anchor algorithm.
In
Figure

1
, we compare the trajectories of
FEG
,
Dual-FEG
, and the Extragradient (EG)
(
Korpelevich, 1976
)
algorithms.

Figure

1(a)
uses a bilinear example
{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)=uv
with
\alpha=0.005
and
N=5000
as an approximation of the algorithms’ behavior in the limit
\alpha\to 0
, and
Figure

1(b)
uses a
non-bilinear example
{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)=u^{2}v
(which is convex-concave and smooth on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
) with
\alpha=0.05
and
N=10000
.
In Figures
2
and
3
,
we plot
\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(x_{k})\right\|^{2}
.

Figure

2(a)
uses a worst-case bilinear example due to
Ouyang & Xu (2021)
:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}u^{\intercal}Gu-g^{\intercal}u-\left\langle Au-b,v\right\rangle

where
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
n=200
.
The precise terms of
{\mathbf{L}}
are stated in
Section

I.2
.
We use initial points
u_{0}=0,v_{0}=0
and use
\alpha=1.0
and
N=10000
.

Figure

2(b)
uses the same example of
Ouyang & Xu (2021)
with additional
\mu
-strongly convex term in
u
and
\mu
-strongly-concave term in
v
(with
\mu=0.1
):

\displaystyle{\mathbf{L}}_{\mu}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}u^{\intercal}(G+\mu I)u-g^{\intercal}u-\left\langle Au-b,v\right\rangle-\frac{\mu}{2}\|v\|^{2}.

Figure

3
considers the linearly constrained convex minimization problem

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}\underset{u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\text{minimize}}&h_{\delta}(u)\\
\text{subject to}&Au=b\end{array}

where
h_{\delta}(u)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}&\text{if}\,\,\|u\|\leq\delta\\
\delta\|u\|-\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}
is the Huber loss and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
(
m<n
).
We run
FEG
and
Dual-FEG
on the Lagrangian
{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)=h_{\delta}(u)+\left\langle Au-b,v\right\rangle
.
We choose
n=100
,
m=20
, randomly generate entries of
A
as i.i.d.
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/n^{2})
, randomly choose
\frac{n}{10}
coordinates where
\overline{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has nonzero values (uniform random in
[0,1]
) and set
b=A\overline{u}
.
We use initial points
u_{0},v_{0}
with i.i.d. standard normal coordinates and choose
\delta=0.1
,
\alpha=0.5
and
N=10^{5}
.

Figure 3
:
Performance of algorithms in reducing
\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(x_{k})\right\|^{2}
for the Lagrangian of a linearly constrained smooth convex minimization problem. The dashed black line indicates the theoretical upper bounds for
FEG
.

The trajectories of
Dual-FEG
are qualitatively different from those of
FEG
, indicating that the two acceleration mechanisms are genuinely different.
Interestingly, however, we find that the last iterates of
Dual-FEG
and
FEG
are identical when the operator is linear, as we show in
Section

I.3
. Indeed, Figures
1(a)
and
2
show the two algorithms arriving at the same point at the terminal iteration. However, this is not true for nonlinear operators; in Figure
1(b)
, we observe that the terminal iterate of
Dual-FEG
does not coincide with the terminal iterate of
FEG
.

In Figures
2(b)
and
3
, we observe that
Dual-FEG
approaches the terminal accuracy much earlier compared to FEG, which progresses much more steadily.
In
Section

I.4
, we prove that in continuous-time, this phenomenon occurs when the objective function is strongly-convex-strongly-concave.
We anticipate that the discrete dual algorithms also exhibit a similar phenomenon, and we leave this investigation to future work.

Comparison of primal and dual algorithms.

We observe that the dual algorithms require
N
or
T
to be specified in advance, while the primal ones do not. In this respect, primal algorithms are advantageous over dual algorithms.
On the other hand, we observe that for problems involving strongly monotone operators, dual algorithms exhibit much faster convergence than primal algorithms in the earlier iterations.
As the primal and dual algorithms share the same worst-case guarantee but display distinct characteristics, determining the best choice of algorithm may require considering other criterion that depend on the particular application scenario.

9
Conclusion

This work presents a new class of accelerations in fixed-point and minimax problems and provides the correct perspective that optimal acceleration is a family of algorithms. Our findings open several new avenues of research on accelerated algorithms. Since the worst-case guarantee, as a criterion, does not uniquely identify a single best method, additional criteria should be introduced to break the tie. Moreover, the role of H-duality in discovering the new dual accelerated algorithms hints at a deeper yet unexplored significance of H-duality in the theory of first-order algorithms.

Impact statement

This paper presents a theoretical study of optimization.
Given its abstract nature, we do not expect our work to raise any significant ethical or societal concerns and consider it neutral in terms of immediate real-world impact.

Appendix A
Related work

Fixed-point algorithms.

Iterative algorithms for solving fixed-point problems (
1.2
) have been extensively studied over a long period, and among them, Picard iteration, Krasnosel’skii–Mann (KM) Iteration and Halpern Iteration stand out as representative classes, each defined by:

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{T}}y_{k},

(Picard)

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{k+1}y_{k}+(1-\lambda_{k+1}){\mathbb{T}}y_{k},

(KM)

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{k+1}y_{0}+(1-\lambda_{k+1}){\mathbb{T}}y_{k}

(Halpern)

The formal study of
Picard
iteration dates up to
Banach (1922)
.
The class
KM
generalizes the works of
Krasnosel’skii (1955)
and
Mann (1953)
; early works
(
Ishikawa, 1976
;
Borwein et al., 1992
)
focused on its asymptotic convergence
\left\|y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right\|^{2}\to 0
, while quantitative rates of
\mathcal{O}(1/k)
to
o(1/k)
were respectively demonstrated by
Cominetti et al. (2014)
;
Liang et al. (2016)
;
Bravo & Cominetti (2018)
and
Baillon & Bruck (1992)
;
Davis & Yin (2016)
;
Matsushita (2017)
.
For the
Halpern
class, devised by
Halpern (1967)
,
Wittmann (1992)
;
Xu (2002)
established asymptotic convergence. Concerning quantitative convergence,
(
Leustean, 2007
)
provided the rate
\left\|y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1/(\log k)^{2})
, which was later improved to
\mathcal{O}(1/k)

(
Kohlenbach, 2011
)
, and to
\mathcal{O}(1/k^{2})
, first by
Sabach & Shtern (2017)
, and then by
Lieder (2021)
with a tighter constant, using the choice
\lambda_{k}=\frac{1}{k+1}
.

Park & Ryu (2022)
established the exact optimality of the convergence rate from
Lieder (2021)
(and the independently discovered equivalent result of
Kim (2021)
) by constructing a matching complexity lower bound.
Some notable acceleration results for fixed-point problems not covered by the above list include Anderson-type acceleration
(
Anderson, 1965
;
Walker & Ni, 2011
;
Zhang et al., 2020
)
and inertial-type acceleration
(
Maingé, 2008
;
Dong et al., 2018
;
Shehu, 2018
;
Reich et al., 2021
)
.

Minimax optimization algorithms.

Smooth convex-concave minimax optimization is a classical problem, whose investigation dates up to
Korpelevich (1976)
;
Popov (1980)
, which respectively first developed the Extragradient (EG) and optimistic gradient (OG) whose variants have been studied extensively
(
Solodov & Svaiter, 1999
;
Nemirovski, 2004
;
Nesterov, 2007
;
Rakhlin & Sridharan, 2013
;
Daskalakis et al., 2018
)
across the optimization and machine learning communities.
Recently there has been rapid development in the theory of reducing
\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(\cdot)\right\|^{2}
; while EG and OG converge at the rate of
\left\|\nabla{\mathbf{L}}(u_{k},v_{k})\right\|^{2}={\mathcal{O}}(1/k)

(
Gorbunov et al., 2022
;
Cai et al., 2022
)
, using the anchoring mechanism
(
Ryu et al., 2019
)
motivated by the optimal Halpern iteration
(
Halpern, 1967
;
Kim, 2021
;
Lieder, 2021
)
,
Diakonikolas (2020)
achieved the near-optimal rate
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(1/k^{2})
, which was finally accelerated to
{\mathcal{O}}(1/k^{2})
via Extra Anchored Gradient (EAG) algorithm
(
Yoon & Ryu, 2021
)
.

Lee & Kim (2021)
provided a constant factor improvement over EAG while generalizing the acceleration to weakly nonconvex-nonconcave problems, and
Tran-Dinh & Luo (2021)
;
Cai & Zheng (2023)
presented single-call versions of the acceleration.

Boţ et al. (2023)
achieved asymptotic
o(1/k^{2})
convergence based on the continuous-time perspective.
The conceptual connection between smooth minimax optimization and fixed-point problems (proximal algorithms) have been formally studied in
Mokhtari et al. (2020)
;
Yoon & Ryu (2022)
.

Continuous-time analyses.

Taking the continuous-time limit of an iterative algorithm results in an ordinary differential equation (ODE), and they often more easily reveal the structure of convergence analysis.
The ODE models for OG and EG were respectively studied by
Csetnek et al. (2019)
;
Lu (2022)
.
The continuous-time analysis of accelerated algorithms was initiated by
Su et al. (2014)
;
Krichene et al. (2015)
, and the anchor acceleration ODE for fixed-point and minimax problems was first introduced by
Ryu et al. (2019)
and
then generalized to a broader family of differential inclusion with more rigorous treatment by
Suh et al. (2023)
.

Boţ et al. (2023)
studied a different family of ODE that achieves acceleration asymptotically.
An ODE involving the coefficient of the form
\frac{1}{T-t}
with fixed terminal time
T
was first presented in
Suh et al. (2022)
,
as continuous-time model of the OGM-G algorithm
(
Kim & Fessler, 2021
)
.
The formal concept of H-duality in continuous-time has been proposed by
Kim et al. (2023a)
, while
Kim & Yang (2023a)
;
Kim & Yang (2023b)
also presents a similar result.

Performance estimation problem (PEP) technique.

At a high level, the PEP methodology
(
Drori & Teboulle, 2014
;
Taylor et al., 2017
;
Taylor & Bach, 2019
;
Ryu et al., 2020
;
Das Gupta et al., 2023
)
provides a computer-assisted framework for finding tight convergence proofs and optimizing the step-sizes of optimization of algorithms, and many efficient algorithms and novel proofs have recently been discovered with the aid of this methodology
(
Kim & Fessler, 2016
;
Lieder, 2021
;
Kim, 2021
;
Kim & Fessler, 2021
;
Park & Ryu, 2021
;
Park & Ryu, 2022
;
Gorbunov et al., 2022
;
Taylor & Drori, 2022
;
Jang et al., 2023
;
Park & Ryu, 2023
)
.

Specifically relevant to our work are the prior, concurrent work of
Kim (2021)
presenting APPM and of
Lieder (2021)
presenting OHM.
It was later shown that APPM and OHM are equivalent, and they represent one element within the family of exact optimal algorithms that we present in this work. We find it somewhat surprising that the two authors independently found the same algorithm when there is an infinitude of answers. Therefore, we personally asked Kim and Lieder to understand how their processes led to their discovery and not any other choice.

In a personal communication, Donghwan Kim explained that the search process in
(
Kim & Fessler, 2016
;
Kim, 2021
)
, at a high level, involved the following steps:

1.

Identify the set of inequalities needed to establish a convergence guarantee for a simpler “reference algorithm” (such as gradient descent or proximal point method).

2.

Set the algorithm step-sizes as additional variables within the PEP formulation, and perform joint minimization of the convergence rate (worst-case complexity) with respect to the step-sizes. This may result in a nonconvex formulation, requiring heuristics such as alternating minimization and repetition of local minimization followed by perturbation (to escape from local optimum).

3.

Iterate Step 2 while restricting the set of inequalities available within the proof (that PEP formulation uses) based on the pattern detected from Step 1. This often facilitates convergence to the global minimum and encourages numerically cleaner solutions that are conducive to analysis by humans.
In the search of APPM
(
Kim, 2021
)
, for instance, only the inequalities
\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{i+1}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{i},x_{i+1}-x_{i}\right\rangle\geq 0
for
i=1,\dots,N-1
and
\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N},x_{N}-y_{\star}\right\rangle\geq 0
were used.

While the strategy of using a constrained set of inequalities is effective for identifying
an
optimal algorithm, it would prevent the discovery of other optimal algorithms that require distinct proof structures.

In another personal communication, Felix Lieder explained that the search process of OHM in
Lieder (2021)
involved the optimization of step-sizes as variables within the PEP formulation (as in the Step 2 in the case of APPM), but unlike
(
Kim, 2021
)
, the entire set of available inequalities was used (without restriction).
Local nonlinear programming solvers were employed to tackle this nonconvex optimization, and within multiple trials with random initialization, the step-sizes often converged near the step-size values for OHM (as detailed in
(
Lieder, 2018
)
).
In this case, despite the numerical indication that optimal step-sizes were not unique, the authors’ main goal was to identify the simplest, hence most easily interpretable solution, which turned out to be OHM in the end.

In our work, we take a mixture of deductive and numerical approaches.
Our initial motivation to investigate the H-dual of OHM (namely
Dual-OHM
) came from the convex H-duality theory
(
Kim et al., 2023a
)
, and PEP was mainly utilized for quick search of proofs (i.e. the correct way of combining the inequalities) and for numerical verification of the convergence guarantees.

Non-uniqueness of exact optimal algorithms.

In the setup of minimizing a non-smooth convex function
f
whose subgradient magnitude is bounded by
M
(so the objective function is
M
-Lipschitz continuous), there are at least 4 different algorithms achieving the exact optimal complexity.
Suppose that
\|x_{0}-x_{\star}\|\leq R
, where
x_{\star}
is a minimizer of
f
.
The subgradient method of
(
Nesterov, 2004
)
, given by
x_{k+1}=x_{k}-\frac{R}{\sqrt{N+1}}\frac{g_{k}}{\left\|g_{k}\right\|}
where
g_{k}\in\partial f(x_{k})
, exhibits the rate
f(x_{N})-f_{\star}\leq\frac{MR}{\sqrt{N+1}}
where
f_{\star}=f(x_{\star})
.
The exact same rate is achieved by the algorithm of
(
Drori & Teboulle, 2016
)
(which is a variant of the cutting-plane method), a fixed step-size algorithm of
(
Drori & Taylor, 2020
)
(without gradient normalization), and its line-search variant.
As there is a matching lower bound
f(x_{N})-f_{\star}\geq\frac{MR}{\sqrt{N+1}}

(
Drori & Teboulle, 2016
)
, all these methods are exactly optimal in terms of the worst-case complexity.

In the fixed-point setup (or the equivalent monotone inclusion setup), a Halpern-type algorithm by
Suh et al. (2023)
that uses adaptive interpolation (anchoring) coefficients also achieves the exact optimal complexity (same rate as
OHM
).
Hence, in a strict sense, our work is not the first discovery of the non-uniqueness of exact optimal algorithms.
However, to the best of our knowledge, our work is the first to identify distinct
fixed step-size
algorithms sharing the same exact optimal complexity for fixed-point problems.
Additionally, while the adaptive algorithm of
(
Suh et al., 2023
)
relies on a mechanism that is similar to
OHM
, our dual algorithms use an arguably different mechanism from that underlying
OHM
.

Appendix B
Equivalence of algorithms

In this section, we show equivalences between distinct forms of algorithms.

B.1
OHM

Form 1
(Anchoring form).

\displaystyle y_{k+1}=\frac{k+1}{k+2}{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}+\frac{1}{k+2}y_{0}

(9)

Form 2
(Momentum form).

\displaystyle y_{k+1}=y_{k}-\frac{1}{k+2}(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k})+\frac{k}{k+2}\left({\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}\right)

(10)

where
{\mathbb{T}}y_{-1}=x_{0}
.

Form 3
(H-matrix form).

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}h_{k+1,j+1}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})

where

\displaystyle h_{k,j}=\begin{cases}-\frac{j}{k(k+1)}&\text{if }j<k,\\
\frac{k}{k+1}&\text{if }j=k.\end{cases}

(11)

Form 4
(APPM).

\displaystyle\begin{split}x_{k+1}&={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}(y_{k})\\
y_{k+1}&=x_{k+1}+\frac{k}{k+2}(x_{k+1}-x_{k})-\frac{k}{k+2}(x_{k}-y_{k-1})\end{split}

(12)

where
y_{-1}=x_{0}=y_{0}
and
{\mathbb{A}}=2({\mathbb{T}}+{\mathbb{I}})^{-1}-{\mathbb{I}}\iff{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
.

Form 1
\implies
Form 2.

Multiplying
k+2
to (
9
) gives

\displaystyle(k+2)y_{k+1}=(k+1){\mathbb{T}}y_{k}+y_{0}.

Thus
y_{0}=(k+2)y_{k+1}-(k+1){\mathbb{T}}y_{k}
.
Substituting this into
y_{0}=(k+1)y_{k}-k{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}
and rearranging:

\displaystyle 0=(k+2)y_{k+1}-(k+1){\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-(k+1)y_{k}+k{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}\iff\text{[Form 2]}.

Form 2
\iff
Form 4.

Direct substitution
x_{k+1}=\frac{1}{2}(y_{k}+{\mathbb{T}}y_{k})
(which follows from
{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}=\frac{1}{2}({\mathbb{I}}+{\mathbb{T}})
) shows that (
10
) and (
12
) are the identical update rules.

Form 4
\implies
Form 1.

Multiplying
k+2
throughout the second line of (
12
) gives

\displaystyle(k+2)y_{k+1}=(2k+2)x_{k+1}-2kx_{k}+ky_{k-1}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle(k+2)y_{k+1}-ky_{k-1}=(2k+2)x_{k+1}-2kx_{k}.

Summing up the last line, we see that the terms telescope:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left[(j+2)y_{j+1}-jy_{j-1}\right]=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left[(2j+2)x_{j+1}-2jx_{j}\right]

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle(k+2)y_{k+1}+(k+1)y_{k}-y_{0}=(2k+2)x_{k+1}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle(k+2)y_{k+1}=y_{0}+(k+1)(2x_{k+1}-y_{k})=y_{0}+(k+1)(2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}y_{k}-y_{k})

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle y_{k+1}=\frac{1}{k+2}y_{0}+\frac{k+1}{k+2}(2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}})(y_{k})=\frac{k+1}{k+2}{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}+\frac{1}{k+2}y_{0}.

The last line is precisely (
9
).

Form 1
\implies
Form 3.

Observe that
y_{1}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{T}}y_{0}+\frac{1}{2}y_{0}=y_{0}-\frac{1}{2}(y_{0}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{0})
, so we have
h_{1,1}=\frac{1}{2}
, which is agrees with (
11
).
We use induction on
k
: Let
k\geq 1
and suppose that (
9
) satisfies the representation (
11
) up to
y_{0},\dots,y_{k}
.
Then

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{k+1}{k+2}{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}+\frac{1}{k+2}y_{0}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\frac{k+1}{k+2}(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k})+\frac{1}{k+2}(y_{0}-y_{k}),

(13)

and

\displaystyle y_{0}-y_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(y_{i-1}-y_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}h_{i,j+1}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{i-2}-\frac{j+1}{i(i+1)}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})+\frac{i}{i+1}(y_{i-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{i-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}-\frac{j+1}{(i+1)(i+2)}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})\right)+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{j+1}{j+2}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{k+1}(y_{k-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1})+\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\left(\frac{j+1}{j+2}-\sum_{i=j+1}^{k-1}\frac{j+1}{(i+1)(i+2)}\right)(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})

(14)

where we change the order of double summation to obtain the last identity.
Applying the following formula

\displaystyle\sum_{i=j+1}^{k-1}\frac{j+1}{(i+1)(i+2)}=(j+1)\sum_{i=j+1}^{k-1}\left(\frac{1}{i+1}-\frac{1}{i+2}\right)=(j+1)\left(\frac{1}{j+2}-\frac{1}{k+1}\right)

to (
14
) gives

\displaystyle y_{0}-y_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{k+1}(y_{k-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1})+\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\frac{j+1}{k+1}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{j+1}{k+1}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j}).

Finally, plugging the last expression into (
13
), we obtain

\displaystyle y_{k+1}=y_{k}-\frac{k+1}{k+2}(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k})+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{j+1}{(k+1)(k+2)}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j}),

so
h_{k+1,j+1}=-\frac{j+1}{(k+1)(k+2)}
for
j=0,\dots,k-1
and
h_{k+1,k+1}=\frac{k+1}{k+2}
, completing the induction.

Form 3
\implies
Form 4.

We have

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}y_{0}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{T}}y_{0}=\frac{1}{2}y_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(2x_{1}-y_{0})=x_{1},

which agrees with (
12
) with
k=0
.

Now let
k\geq 1
.
Let
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}=y_{j}-x_{j+1}
for
j=0,1,\dots
.
Then
y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j}=y_{j}-(2x_{j+1}-y_{j})=2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}
, and

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}2h_{k+1,j+1}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{2(j+1)}{(k+1)(k+2)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}-\frac{2(k+1)}{k+2}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1},

(15)

and

\displaystyle x_{k+1}-x_{k}

\displaystyle=(y_{k}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1})-(y_{k-1}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k})

\displaystyle=y_{k}-y_{k-1}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}+\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}2h_{k,j+1}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}+\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\frac{2(j+1)}{k(k+1)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}+\left(1-\frac{2k}{k+1}\right)\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\frac{2(j+1)}{k(k+1)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}-\frac{k-1}{k+1}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}.

Thus,

\displaystyle x_{k+1}+\frac{k}{k+2}(x_{k+1}-x_{k})-\frac{k}{k+2}(x_{k}-y_{k-1})

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}+\frac{k}{k+2}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\frac{2(j+1)}{k(k+1)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}-\frac{k-1}{k+1}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}\right)+\frac{k}{k+2}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}

\displaystyle=y_{k}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\frac{2(j+1)}{(k+1)(k+2)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}+\left(\frac{k}{k+2}-\frac{k(k-1)}{(k+1)(k+2)}\right)\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}-\frac{2k+2}{k+2}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{2(j+1)}{(k+1)(k+2)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}-\frac{2(k+1)}{k+2}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k+1}

and the proof is complete (the last line follows from (
15
)).

B.2
Dual-OHM

Form 1
(Momentum form).

\displaystyle y_{k+1}=y_{k}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left({\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}\right)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
, where
{\mathbb{T}}y_{-1}=y_{0}
.

Form 2
(
z
-form).

\displaystyle\begin{split}z_{k+1}&=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-\frac{1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right)\\
y_{k+1}&={\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-z_{k+1}\end{split}

(16)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
, where
z_{0}=0
.

Form 3
(H-matrix form).

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}h_{k+1,j+1}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}2h_{k+1,j+1}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
, where

\displaystyle h_{k,j}=\begin{cases}-\frac{N-k}{(N-j)(N-j+1)}&\text{if }j<k,\\
\frac{N-k}{N-k+1}&\text{if }j=k.\end{cases}

(17)

Form 4
(Proximal form of
Section

3.2
).

\displaystyle\begin{split}x_{k+1}&={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}(y_{k})\\
y_{k+1}&=x_{k+1}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(x_{k+1}-x_{k})-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(x_{k}-y_{k-1})-\frac{1}{N-k}(x_{k+1}-y_{k})\end{split}

(18)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
,
where
x_{0}=y_{0}
and
{\mathbb{A}}=2({\mathbb{T}}+{\mathbb{I}})^{-1}-{\mathbb{I}}\iff{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
.

Form 1
\implies
Form 2.

Let
z_{0}=0
and
z_{k}={\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}-y_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,N-1
, so that the second line of (
16
) holds by definition.
Following establishes the recursion for
z_{k}
, which is the first line of (
16
):

\displaystyle z_{k+1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-y_{k+1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-\left(y_{k}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}({\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1})\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-\left(y_{k}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}({\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-z_{k}-y_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-\frac{1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right).

Form 2
\implies
Form 3.

The first line of (
16
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle z_{j+1}=\frac{N-j-1}{N-j}z_{j}-\frac{2}{N-j}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}.

Dividing both sides by
N-j-1
and rearranging, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N-j-1}z_{j+1}-\frac{1}{N-j}z_{j}=-\frac{2}{(N-j)(N-j-1)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}.

Summing this up from
j=0
to
k
and multiplying
N-k-1
to the both sides we have

\displaystyle z_{k+1}=-(N-k-1)\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{2}{(N-j)(N-j-1)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}

(note that
z_{0}=0
).
Next, we substitute the last expression into the second line of (
16
):

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}+({\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-y_{k})-z_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}+(N-k-1)\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{2}{(N-j)(N-j-1)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{2(N-k-1)}{(N-j+1)(N-j)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j}.

This shows that
h_{k+1,j}=-\frac{(N-k-1)}{(N-j)(N-j+1)}
for
j=1,\dots,k
and
h_{k+1,k+1}=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}
, which agrees with (
17
).

Form 3
\implies
Form 4.

From the definition of Form 3,

\displaystyle\begin{split}y_{k+1}&=y_{k}-\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{2(N-k-1)}{(N-j-1)(N-j)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}.\end{split}

(19)

Putting
k-1
in place of
k
, we have

\displaystyle y_{k}

\displaystyle=y_{k-1}-\frac{2(N-k)}{N-k+1}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\frac{2(N-k)}{(N-j-1)(N-j)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k-1}-2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{2(N-k)}{(N-j-1)(N-j)}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}.

Using the last equation, we replace the summation within (
19
):

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-y_{k-1}+2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}\right).

Finally, substitute
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}=y_{k}-x_{k+1}
,
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}=y_{k-1}-x_{k}
and rearrange to obtain Form 4:

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-x_{k+1}\right)+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-y_{k-1}+2(y_{k-1}-x_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=x_{k+1}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(x_{k+1}-x_{k})-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(x_{k}-y_{k-1})-\frac{1}{N-k}(x_{k+1}-y_{k}).

Form 4
\implies
Form 1.

Simply substitute
x_{k+1}=\frac{1}{2}(y_{k}+{\mathbb{T}}y_{k})
into the second line of (
18
) and rearrange. In detail:

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=x_{k+1}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(x_{k+1}-x_{k})-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(x_{k}-y_{k-1})-\frac{1}{N-k}(x_{k+1}-y_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}x_{k+1}-\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}x_{k}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}y_{k-1}+\frac{1}{N-k}y_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}+{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right)-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left(y_{k-1}+{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}\right)+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}y_{k-1}+\frac{1}{N-k}y_{k}

\displaystyle=y_{k}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left({\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k-1}\right).

B.3
FEG

Form 1
(Original form).

\displaystyle\begin{split}x_{k+1/2}&=x_{k}+\frac{1}{k+1}(x_{0}-x_{k})-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})\\
x_{k+1}&=x_{k}+\frac{1}{k+1}(x_{0}-x_{k})-\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})\end{split}

(20)

Form 2
(H-matrix form).

\displaystyle x_{(\ell+1)/2}=x_{\ell/2}-\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}h_{(\ell+1)/2,i/2}\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{i/2})

where for
k=0,1,\dots,N-1
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha L}h_{(\ell+1)/2,i/2}=\begin{cases}\frac{k}{k+1}&\text{if }\ell=2k,\,i=2k\\
-\frac{j+1}{k(k+1)}&\text{if }\ell=2k,\,i=2j+1,\,j=0,\dots,k-1\\
0&\text{if }\ell=2k,\,i=2j,\,j=0,\dots,k-1\\
1&\text{if }\ell=2k+1,\,i=2k+1\\
-\frac{k}{k+1}&\text{if }\ell=2k+1,\,i=2k\\
0&\text{if }\ell=2k+1,\,i=0,\dots,2k-1.\end{cases}

(21)

It suffices to inductively check that the two forms indicate the same update rule of generating
x_{k+1/2},x_{k+1}
provided that they are equivalent for all
x_{0},\dots,x_{k}
.
They are clearly equivalent for
k=0
.
Assume that the equivalence holds for
j=0,\dots,k
.
Now we show that the update rule by Form 1 agrees with (
21
).
Multiplying
k+1
to the second line of (
20
) and switching the index from
k
to
j
, we have

\displaystyle(j+1)x_{j+1}=jx_{j}+x_{0}-\alpha(j+1)\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{j+1/2}).

Summing this up from
j=0
to
k
and dividing by
k+1
we have

\displaystyle x_{k+1}-x_{0}=-\frac{1}{k+1}\alpha\sum_{j=0}^{k}(j+1)\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{i+1/2}).

(22)

Also, by subtracting the first line of (
20
) from the second line of (
20
), we obtain

\displaystyle x_{k+1}=x_{k+1/2}-\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})+\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k}).

Then, by applying (
22
) with
k
and
k\leftarrow k-1
we can write

\displaystyle x_{k+1/2}-x_{k}

\displaystyle=x_{k+1}+\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})-x_{k}

\displaystyle=(x_{k+1}-x_{k})+\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})

\displaystyle=(x_{k+1}-x_{0})-(x_{k}-x_{0})+\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{1}{k+1}\alpha\sum_{j=0}^{k}(j+1)\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{i+1/2})\right)-\left(-\frac{1}{k}\alpha\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(j+1)\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{i+1/2})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})

\displaystyle=-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(\frac{j+1}{k}-\frac{j+1}{k+1}\right)\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{j+1/2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{j+1}{k(k+1)}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{j+1/2}).

This shows that

\displaystyle h_{k+1/2,k}=\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha L,\quad h_{k+1/2,j+1/2}=-\frac{j+1}{k(k+1)}\alpha L,\quad h_{k+1/2,j}=0\quad(j=0,\dots,k-1).

Next, because

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=x_{k+1/2}-\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})+\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})

we obtain

\displaystyle h_{k+1,k+1/2}=\alpha L,\quad h_{k+1,k}=-\frac{k}{k+1}\alpha L,\quad h_{k+1,i/2}=0\quad(i=0,\dots,2k-1)

which agrees with (
21
).

B.4
Dual-FEG

Form 1
(Original form).

\displaystyle\begin{split}x_{k+1/2}&=x_{k}-\alpha z_{k}-\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})\\
x_{k+1}&=x_{k+1/2}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha\left(\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})-\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})\right)\\
z_{k+1}&=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-\frac{1}{N-k}\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})\end{split}

(23)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-1
, where
z_{0}=0
.

Form 2
(H-matrix form).

\displaystyle x_{(\ell+1)/2}=x_{\ell/2}-\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}h_{(\ell+1)/2,i/2}\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{i/2})

where

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha L}h_{(\ell+1)/2,i/2}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\ell=2k,\,i=2k\\
-\frac{N-k}{(N-j-1)(N-j)}&\text{if }\ell=2k,\,i=2j+1,\,j=0,\dots,k-1\\
0&\text{if }\ell=2k,\,i=2j,\,j=0,\dots,k-1\\
\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}&\text{if }\ell=2k+1,\,i=2k+1\\
-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}&\text{if }\ell=2k+1,\,i=2k\\
0&\text{if }\ell=2k+1,\,i=0,\dots,2k-1.\end{cases}

(24)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-1
.

As in the case of FEG, we check that update rule (
23
) of Form 1 defines the identical update rule for
x_{k+1/2},x_{k+1}
provided that they are equivalent for all
x_{0},\dots,x_{k}
.
Subtracting the first line of (
23
) from the second line of (
23
) we get

\displaystyle x_{k+1}=x_{k+1/2}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha\left(\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})-\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})\right).

Dividing the the third line of (
23
) by
N-k
and switching the index from
k
to
j
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N-j}z_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N-j+1}z_{j-1}-\frac{1}{(N-j)(N-j+1)}\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{j-1/2}).

Summing this up from
j=0
to
k-1
and multiplying
N-k
to both sides gives

\displaystyle z_{k}=-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{N-k}{(N-j-1)(N-j)}\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{j+1/2})

(note that
z_{0}=0
).
Now substituting the above expression for
z_{k}
into the first line of (
23
) gives

\displaystyle x_{k+1/2}

\displaystyle=x_{k}-\alpha z_{k}-\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})

\displaystyle=x_{k}-\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{N-k}{(N-j-1)(N-j)}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{j+1/2})

and thus,

\displaystyle h_{k+1/2,k}=\alpha L,\quad h_{k+1/2,j+1/2}=-\frac{N-k}{(N-j-1)(N-j)}\alpha L,\quad h_{k+1/2,j}=0\quad(j=0,\dots,k-1).

Finally, the second line of (
23
) is

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=x_{k+1/2}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2})+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})

which gives

\displaystyle h_{k+1,k+1/2}=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha L,\quad h_{k+1,k}=-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha L,\quad h_{k+1,i/2}=0\quad(i=0,\dots,2k-1).

This is precisely (
24
).

B.5
Anchor ODE (
4
)

Form 1
(Original form).

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-\frac{1}{t}(X(t)-X_{0})

(25)

where
X(0)=X_{0}
.

Form 2
(Second-order form).

\displaystyle\ddot{X}(t)+\frac{2}{t}\dot{X}(t)+\frac{1}{t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))+\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=0

(26)

where
X(0)=X_{0}
and
\dot{X}(0)=-A(X_{0})
.

Form 1
\implies
Form 2.

Let
X\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the solution to (
25
) with initial condition
X(0)=X_{0}
.
First observe that taking the limit
t\to 0^{+}
in (
25
) gives
\dot{X}(0)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})-\dot{X}(0)
, which is equivalent to the initial velocity condition
\dot{X}(0)=-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})
for (
26
).
Differentiating both sides of (
25
), we have

\displaystyle\ddot{X}(t)=-\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-\frac{1}{t}\dot{X}(t)+\frac{1}{t^{2}}(X(t)-X_{0}).

Rearranging the defining equation (
25
) gives
\frac{1}{t^{2}}(X(t)-X_{0})=-\frac{1}{t}\dot{X}(t)-\frac{1}{t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
.
Substituting this into the last equation and reorganizing, we obtain (
26
):

\displaystyle\ddot{X}(t)=-\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-\frac{1}{t}\dot{X}(t)+\left(-\frac{1}{t}\dot{X}(t)-\frac{1}{t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right)

\displaystyle\iff\ddot{X}(t)+\frac{2}{t}\dot{X}(t)+\frac{1}{t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))+\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=0.

Form 2
\implies
Form 1.

Suppose
X\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the solution to (
26
) with initial conditions
X(0)=X_{0}
and
\dot{X}(0)=-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})
.
Multiplying
t
throughout (
26
), we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=t\ddot{X}(t)+2\dot{X}(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))+t\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=\frac{d}{dt}\left(t\dot{X}(t)\right)+\dot{X}(t)+\frac{d}{dt}\left(t{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right).

Integrating both sides from
0
to
t
gives

\displaystyle 0=t\dot{X}(t)+X(t)-X_{0}+t{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)).

Dividing both sides by
t
and reorganizing, we get (
25
).

The above result holds given a minimal assumption that
{\mathbb{A}}
is Lipschitz continuous (with the equalities holding for almost every
t
if differentiability is not assumed).
For the rigorous discussion on this point, we refer the readers to
(
Suh et al., 2023
, Appendix B)
.

B.6
Dual-Anchor ODE (
5
)

Form 1
(Original form).

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dot{X}(t)&=-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\\
\dot{Z}(t)&=-\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\end{aligned}

(27)

where
X(0)=X_{0}
and
Z(0)=0
.

Form 2
(Second-order form).

\displaystyle\ddot{X}(t)+\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)-\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=0

(28)

where
X(0)=X_{0}
and
\dot{X}(0)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})
.

Form 1
\implies
Form 2.

Let
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the solution to (
27
) with initial conditions
X(0)=X_{0}
and
Z(0)=0
.
Plugging
t=0
into the first line of (
5
) gives
\dot{X}(0)=0-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})
, which is the initial velocity condition for (
28
).
Now observe that

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(t)=\frac{1}{T-t}\left(-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right)=\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)

where the last equality comes from the first line of (
27
).
Differentiating the first line of (
27
) and plugging in the above identity we obtain Form 2:

\displaystyle 0=\ddot{X}(t)+\dot{Z}(t)+\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=\ddot{X}(t)+\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)-\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)).

Form 2
\implies
Form 1.

Suppose
X\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the solution to (
28
) with initial conditions
X(0)=X_{0}
and
\dot{X}(0)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})
.
Define
Z\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
Z(t)=-\dot{X}(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
.
Then
\dot{X}(t)=-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
by definition (this is the first line of (
27
)).
Also note that
Z(0)=-\dot{X}(0)-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})=0
, which is the
Z
-initial condition for (
27
).
Now differentiating
Z
we have

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(t)=-\ddot{X}(t)-\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)=-\frac{1}{T-t}\left(Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right).

The second equality directly follows from the defining equation (
28
).
This shows that
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
is the solution of (
27
).

The above result holds given a minimal assumption that
{\mathbb{A}}
is Lipschitz continuous (with the equalities holding for almost every
t
if differentiability is not assumed).
For the rigorous discussion on existence, uniqueness of the solutions and almost everywhere differntiability of the involved quantities, we refer the readers to
Appendix

H
.

Appendix C
Lyapunov analysis of
Dual-OHM

Recall the following form of
Dual-OHM
:

\displaystyle z_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-\frac{1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right)

(29)

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-z_{k+1}.

(30)

Substituting (
29
) into (
30
) we get

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}+\frac{1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N-k}y_{k}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}

(31)

With the substitution
{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
and
x_{k+1}={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}y_{k}
we can write (
29
) and (
31
) as

\displaystyle z_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-\frac{2}{N-k}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}

(32)

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N-k}y_{k}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left(y_{k}-2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}\right)-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}

(33)

where we have used
{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}y_{k}-y_{k}=2x_{k+1}-y_{k}=y_{k}-2(y_{k}-x_{k+1})=y_{k}-2\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k+1}
.
For simplicity, write
g_{j}=\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,N
.
To complete the proof, it remains to show that for

\displaystyle V_{k}=\underbrace{-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left\|z_{k}+2g_{N}\right\|^{2}}_{:=V_{k}^{(1)}}+\underbrace{\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle z_{k}+2g_{N},y_{k}-y_{N-1}\right\rangle}_{:=V_{k}^{(2)}}

the following holds:

\displaystyle V_{k}-V_{k+1}=\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\langle x_{k+1}-x_{N},g_{k+1}-g_{N}\right\rangle.

Rewriting the right hand side, we have

\displaystyle\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\langle x_{k+1}-x_{N},g_{k+1}-g_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\langle y_{k}-y_{N-1}-(g_{k+1}-g_{N}),g_{k+1}-g_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle y_{k}-y_{N-1},\frac{2}{N-k}g_{k+1}-\frac{2}{N-k}g_{N}\right\rangle-\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\|g_{k+1}-g_{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn:appendix-dual-Halpern-z-update-monotone-operator}}}{{=}}\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle y_{k}-y_{N-1},\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}(z_{k}+2g_{N})-(z_{k+1}+2g_{N})\right\rangle-\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\|g_{k+1}-g_{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle y_{k}-y_{N-1},z_{k}+2g_{N}\right\rangle-\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle y_{k}-y_{N-1},z_{k+1}+2g_{N}\right\rangle-\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\|g_{k+1}-g_{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn:appendix-dual-Halpern-y-update-monotone-operator}}}{{=}}V_{k}^{(2)}-\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle y_{k+1}-y_{N-1}+\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}g_{k+1}+\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k},z_{k+1}+2g_{N}\right\rangle-\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\|g_{k+1}-g_{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=V_{k}^{(2)}-V_{k+1}^{(2)}\underbrace{-\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle 2g_{k+1}+z_{k},z_{k+1}+2g_{N}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\|2g_{k+1}-2g_{N}\right\|^{2}}_{:=R_{k}}

and the proof is done once we show
R_{k}=V_{k}^{(1)}-V_{k+1}^{(1)}
.
From (
32
) we have
2g_{k+1}=(N-k-1)z_{k}-(N-k)z_{k+1}
,
and plugging this into
R_{k}
we obtain

\displaystyle R_{k}

\displaystyle=-2\left\langle z_{k+1}-z_{k},z_{k+1}+2g_{N}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\|(N-k-1)z_{k}-(N-k)z_{k+1}-2g_{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\left\langle(z_{k+1}+2g_{N})-(z_{k}+2g_{N}),z_{k+1}+2g_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\|(N-k-1)(z_{k}+2g_{N})-(N-k)(z_{k+1}+2g_{N})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\left\|z_{k+1}+2g_{N}\right\|^{2}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left\|z_{k}+2g_{N}\right\|^{2}-\frac{N-k}{N-k-1}\left\|z_{k+1}+2g_{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left\|z_{k}+2g_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{N-k-2}{N-k-1}\left\|z_{k+1}+2g_{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=V_{k}^{(1)}-V_{k+1}^{(1)},

which proves that indeed,
R_{k}=V_{k}^{(1)}-V_{k+1}^{(1)}
.

Appendix D
Fixed-point H-duality theory

D.1
The precise statement of H-duality theorem

To state the theorem, we first need to set up some notations and concepts.

Primal Lyapunov structure.

Consider the H-matrix representation of an algorithm

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}h_{k+1,j+1}(y_{j}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{j})=y_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}2h_{k+1,j+1}\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}

(34)

for
k=0,1,\dots,N-2
, where
x_{k+1}={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}(y_{k})
for
k=0,1,\dots,N-1
.
Consider a convergence proof for (
34
) with respect to the performance measure
\left\|y_{N-1}-{\mathbb{T}}y_{N-1}\right\|^{2}=4\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}
, structured in the following way:
Take a sequence
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N-1}
of positive numbers, and define the sequence
\{U_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
by
U_{1}=0
and

\displaystyle U_{j+1}=U_{j}-u_{j}\left\langle x_{j+1}-x_{j},\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j+1}-\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{j}\right\rangle

for
j=1,\dots,N-1
.
As
{\mathbb{A}}
is monotone,
\{U_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
is a nonincreasing sequence.
To clarify, while we restrict the proof structure to use a specific combination of monotonicity inequalities, we let
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N-1}
as free variables which can be appropriately chosen to make the convergence analysis work.

Assume that we can show that for some
\tau_{U}>0
,

\displaystyle\tau_{U}\left\|\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}+\left\langle\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N},x_{N}-y_{0}\right\rangle\leq U_{N}

(C1)

holds for arbitrary
\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{1},\dots,\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}
.
To put this precisely,
(\text{RHS})-(\text{LHS})
in (
C1
) is a “vector quadratic form” of
