\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{N}-x_{k+1}\right\rangle-\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{N}-x_{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad-2\alpha\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},\frac{1}{N-k}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}+\frac{1}{N-k}z_{k}\right\rangle-\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle\frac{1}{N-k}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2},x_{N}-x_{k+1/2}\right\rangle

(80)

and plug the following identity (which is the third line of
Dual-FEG
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N-k}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-z_{k+1}

(81)

into (
80
) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{2}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\mathrm{MI}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{N}-x_{k+1}\right\rangle-\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{N}-x_{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad-2\alpha\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},z_{k}-z_{k+1}\right\rangle-\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}z_{k}-z_{k+1},x_{N}-x_{k+1/2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{k}-x_{N}\right\rangle-2\alpha\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},z_{k}\right\rangle-\left(\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{k+1}-x_{N}\right\rangle-2\alpha\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},z_{k+1}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\underbrace{2\left\langle\frac{1}{N-k}z_{k}-\frac{1}{N-k-1}z_{k+1},x_{k+1/2}-x_{N}\right\rangle}_{:=R_{k}}

\displaystyle=V_{k}^{(1)}-V_{k+1}^{(1)}+R_{k}

(82)

We rewrite
R_{k}
as following, using (
79
):

\displaystyle R_{k}

\displaystyle=2\left\langle\frac{1}{N-k}z_{k},x_{k+1/2}-x_{N}\right\rangle-2\left\langle\frac{1}{N-k-1}z_{k+1},x_{k+1/2}-x_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=2\left\langle\frac{1}{N-k}z_{k},x_{k}-x_{N}-\alpha z_{k}-\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right\rangle-2\left\langle\frac{1}{N-k-1}z_{k+1},x_{k+1}-x_{N}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha({\mathbb{A}}x_{k}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle z_{k},x_{k}-x_{N}\right\rangle-\frac{2}{N-k-1}\left\langle z_{k+1},x_{k+1}-x_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{2\alpha}{N-k}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{k},z_{k+1}-z_{k}\right\rangle-\frac{2\alpha}{N-k}\|z_{k}\|^{2}-2\alpha\left\langle z_{k+1},\frac{1}{N-k}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=V_{k}^{(2)}-V_{k+1}^{(2)}+\alpha\left(\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{k},z_{k+1}-z_{k}\right\rangle-\frac{2}{N-k}\|z_{k}\|^{2}-\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}\left\langle z_{k+1},z_{k}\right\rangle+2\left\|z_{k+1}\right\|^{2}\right)

(83)

where the last equality uses (
81
).
Now multiplying
\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)^{2}}
to
\mathrm{LI}_{k}
and applying the identities (
79
), (
81
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)^{2}}\left(\mathrm{LI}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)^{2}}\left(\|x_{k+1/2}-x_{k}\|^{2}-\alpha^{2}\|{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(N-k)^{2}}\left(\alpha\left\|z_{k}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right\|^{2}-\alpha\|(N-k-1)z_{k}-(N-k)z_{k+1}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha\left(\left\|\frac{1}{N-k}z_{k}+\frac{1}{N-k}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right\|^{2}-\left\|\frac{1}{N-k}z_{k}+\frac{1}{N-k}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}+(z_{k+1}-z_{k})\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left\langle z_{k+1}-\left(1-\frac{2}{N-k}\right)z_{k}+\frac{2}{N-k}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k},z_{k+1}-z_{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left(\left\|z_{k+1}\right\|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{2}{N-k}\right)\left\|z_{k}\right\|^{2}-\frac{2(N-k-1)}{N-k}\left\langle z_{k},z_{k+1}\right\rangle+\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{k},z_{k+1}-z_{k}\right\rangle\right)

(84)

holds.
Now, we add (
82
) with (
84
), plug in (
83
) and simplify to obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{2}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left(\mathrm{MI}_{k}\right)+\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)^{2}}\left(\mathrm{LI}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=(V_{k}^{(1)}+V_{k}^{(2)}-\alpha\|z_{k}\|^{2})-(V_{k+1}^{(1)}+V_{k+1}^{(2)}-\alpha\|z_{k+1}\|^{2})

\displaystyle=V_{k}-V_{k+1}.

It remains to show
V_{N-1}\geq 0
.
When
k=N-1
, we have

\displaystyle x_{N}=x_{N-1/2}=x_{N-1}-\alpha z_{N-1}-\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{N-1}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad z_{N-1}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N-1}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(x_{N}-x_{N-1}\right).

Therefore,

\displaystyle V_{N-1}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left\|z_{N-1}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}+2\left\langle z_{N-1}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{N-1}-x_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left\|{z_{N-1}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N-1}}+\left({\mathbb{A}}x_{N}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{N-1}\right)\right\|^{2}+2\left\langle z_{N-1}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N-1},x_{N-1}-x_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left\|-\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(x_{N}-x_{N-1}\right)+\left({\mathbb{A}}x_{N}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{N-1}\right)\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\left\|x_{N}-x_{N-1}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\left\|x_{N}-x_{N-1}\right\|^{2}-\alpha^{2}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{N-1}\right\|^{2}\right)+2\left\langle x_{N}-x_{N-1},{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{N-1}\right\rangle\geq 0,

which concludes the proof.

G.2
Proof of
Proposition

6.2

In
Appendix

B
, we derive the H-matrix forms of
FEG
and
Dual-FEG
, respectively (
21
) and (
24
).
It remains to check that the matrices are indeed in the anti-transpose relationship.
Recall the definition

\displaystyle(H_{\text{Dual-FEG}})_{\ell,i}=\begin{cases}h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}&\text{if }i\leq\ell\\
0&\text{if }i>\ell.\end{cases}

For comparison, write

\displaystyle(H_{\text{FEG}})_{\ell,i}=\begin{cases}\hat{h}_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}&\text{if }i\leq\ell\\
0&\text{if }i>\ell.\end{cases}

We check that
h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}=(H_{\text{Dual-FEG}})_{\ell,i}=(H^{\text{A}}_{\text{FEG}})_{\ell,i}=(H_{\text{FEG}})_{2N-i+1,2N-\ell+1}=\hat{h}_{N-(i-1)/2,N-\ell/2}
by carefully dividing cases, which completes the proof.

Case 1.

For
\ell=i=2k+1
(
k=0,\dots,N-1
),

\displaystyle h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}=h_{k+1/2,k}=\alpha L=\hat{h}_{N-k,N-k-1/2}=\hat{h}_{N-(i-1)/2,N-\ell/2}.

Case 2.

For
\ell=2k+1
(
k=1,\dots,N-1)
,
i=2j+2
(
j=0,\dots,k-1
),

\displaystyle h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}=h_{k+1/2,j+1/2}=-\frac{N-k}{(N-j-1)(N-j)}\alpha L=\hat{h}_{N-j-1/2,N-k-1/2}=\hat{h}_{N-(i-1)/2,N-\ell/2}

(note that
N-j>N-k
).

Case 3.

For
\ell=2k+1
(
k=1,\dots,N-1)
,
i=2j+1
(
j=0,\dots,k-1
),

\displaystyle h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}=h_{k+1/2,j}=0=\hat{h}_{N-j,N-k-1/2}=\hat{h}_{N-(i-1)/2,N-\ell/2}

(note that
N-j>N-k
).

Case 4.

For
\ell=i=2k+2
(
k=0,\dots,N-1
),

\displaystyle h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}=h_{k+1,k+1/2}=\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha L=\hat{h}_{N-k-1/2,N-k-1}=\hat{h}_{N-(i-1)/2,N-\ell/2}.

Case 5.

For
\ell=2k+2
,
i=2k+1
(
k=0,\dots,N-1
),

\displaystyle h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}=h_{k+1,k}=-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha L=\hat{h}_{N-k,N-k-1}=\hat{h}_{N-(i-1)/2,N-\ell/2}.

Case 6.

For
\ell=2k+2
,
i=1,\dots,2k
(
k=0,\dots,N-1
), because
\hat{h}_{k^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=0
for any
j^{\prime}=0,1,\dots
and
k^{\prime}\geq j^{\prime}+3/2
,

\displaystyle h_{\ell/2,(i-1)/2}=h_{k+1,(i-1)/2}=0=\hat{h}_{N-(i-1)/2,N-k-1}=\hat{h}_{N-(i-1)/2,N-\ell/2}.

∎

Appendix H
Omitted details from
Section

7

H.1
Derivation of ODE as continuous-time limit

In this section, we derive the Dual-Anchor ODE as continuous-time limit of
Dual-OHM
and
Dual-FEG
.
The derivation is informal in the sense that we do not rigorously derive the sup-norm convergence

\lim_{\alpha\to 0^{+}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|X(t)-x_{\lfloor t/\alpha\rfloor}\right\|=0,

but we clarify all essential correspondence between discrete and continuous-time quantities.

H.1.1
Dual-OHM
to Dual-Anchor ODE

Assume
{\mathbb{A}}
is continuous monotone operator and let
\alpha>0
.
Consider
Dual-OHM
with
{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}

(note that this differs from the “usual identification”
{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
by scale, because here we need a step-size
\alpha
with respect to which we take the limit).
Let
x_{k+1}={\mathbb{J}}_{\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}}y_{k}
, so that
x_{k+1}+\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}=y_{k}
.
Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{T}}y_{k}=2x_{k+1}-y_{k}=y_{k}-\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1},

and we can rewrite the
z
-form of
Dual-OHM
(
3
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{z_{k+1}}{\alpha}-\frac{z_{k}}{\alpha}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)}\frac{z_{k}}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}

\displaystyle\frac{y_{k+1}-y_{k}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{z_{k+1}}{\alpha}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}.

Identifying
\alpha k=t
,
\alpha N=T
,
y_{k}=Y(t)
,
\frac{z_{k}}{\alpha}=Z(t)
and
x_{k}=X(t)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t+\alpha))

\displaystyle\dot{Y}(t)

\displaystyle=-Z(t+\alpha)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t+\alpha)).

Assuming differentiability of
X,Y,Z
and taking the limit
\alpha\to 0
gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{Z}(t)&=-\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\\
\dot{Y}(t)&=-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)).\end{split}

(85)

Once we show
\dot{Y}(t)=\dot{X}(t)
in the limit
\alpha\to 0
, (
85
) becomes the Dual-Anchor ODE (
5
).
From
x_{k+1}+\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}=y_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)=\frac{x_{k+1}-x_{k}}{\alpha}=\frac{y_{k}-y_{k-1}}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\left({\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right)=\dot{Y}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)-\frac{1}{2}\left({\mathbb{A}}(X(t+\alpha))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right)

which shows that indeed,
\dot{X}(t)=\dot{Y}(t)
in the limit
\alpha\to 0
.

H.1.2
Dual-FEG
to Dual-Anchor ODE

From the definition of
Dual-FEG
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}x_{k+1}&=x_{k+1/2}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha\left({\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right)\\
&=x_{k}-\alpha z_{k}-\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\alpha\left({\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right)\end{split}

(86)

where
{\mathbb{A}}=\nabla_{\pm}{\mathbf{L}}
.
Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\frac{x_{k+1}-x_{k}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-z_{k}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}-\frac{\alpha(N-k-1)}{\alpha(N-k)}\left({\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{z_{k+1}-z_{k}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)}z_{k}-\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}.

Now identify
\alpha k=t
,
\alpha N=T
,
x_{k}=X(t)
and
z_{k}=Z(t)
to obtain

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)

\displaystyle=-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-\frac{T-t-\alpha}{T-t}\left({\mathbb{A}}(X(t)+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right)

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha))

where we use
x_{k+1/2}=x_{k}+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)
.
Thus, under the limit
\alpha\to 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)

\displaystyle=-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))

which is the Dual-Anchor ODE (
5
).

H.2
Proof of
Proposition

7.2

The Anchor ODE (
4
)
(
Ryu et al., 2019
;
Suh et al., 2023
)
is

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-\frac{1}{t}(X(t)-X_{0})

(87)

where
X(0)=X_{0}
.
We first rewrite (
87
) in the H-kernel form

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}H(t,s){\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds.

Multiply
t
to both sides of (
87
) and reorganize to get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(t(X(t)-X_{0})\right)=t\dot{X}(t)+(X(t)-X_{0})=-t{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)).

Integrating from
0
to
t
and dividing both sides by
t
we obtain

\displaystyle X(t)-X_{0}=-\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}s{\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds.

Differentiating both sides gives

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)=\frac{1}{t^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}s{\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{s}{t^{2}}-\delta(s-t)\right){\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds=-\int_{0}^{t}H(t,s){\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds

with

H(t,s)=-\frac{s}{t^{2}}+\delta(s-t)

and
\delta(\cdot)
is the Dirac delta function.
The continuous-time anti-diagonal transpose of this H-kernel is:

\displaystyle H^{\text{A}}(t,s)=H(T-s,T-t)=-\frac{T-t}{(T-s)^{2}}+\delta(s-t),

Therefore, the H-dual of the Anchor ODE is

\displaystyle\dot{X}(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}H^{\text{A}}(t,s){\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{T-t}{(T-s)^{2}}-\delta(s-t)\right){\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{T-t}{(T-s)^{2}}{\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)).

Now define

\displaystyle Z(t)=-\dot{X}(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=-(T-t)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{(T-s)^{2}}{\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds.

Differentiating both sides of the above equation gives

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{(T-s)^{2}}{\mathbb{A}}(X(s))ds-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=-\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)-\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)).

The last equation, together
\dot{X}(t)=-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
which follows directly from definition of
Z
, becomes (
5
), which is the Dual-Anchor ODE.
This shows that Dual-Anchor ODE and Anchor ODE are H-duals of each other.

H.3
Existence, uniqueness and Lyapunov analysis of Dual Anchor ODE

H.3.1
Well-posedness of the dynamics

Theorem H.1
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Lipschitz continuous and let
T>0
.
Consider the differential equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}\dot{X}(t)\\
\dot{Z}(t)\end{pmatrix}&=-\begin{pmatrix}Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\\
\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)+\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(88)

for
t\in(0,T)
, with initial conditions
X(0)=X_{0}
and
Z(0)=0
.
Then there is a unique solution
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,T),\mathbb{R}^{2d})
that satisfies (
88
) for all
t\in(0,T)
.

Proof.

The right hand side of (
88
), as a function of
t
and
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
, can be rewritten as

\displaystyle F\left(t,\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=-\begin{pmatrix}Z+{\mathbb{A}}(X)\\
\frac{1}{T-t}Z+\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X)\end{pmatrix}=-\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&\frac{1}{T-t}I\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}Z+{\mathbb{A}}(X)\\
Z+{\mathbb{A}}(X)\end{pmatrix}.

Let
L>0
be the Lipschitz continuity parameter for
{\mathbb{A}}
.
Fix
\overline{t}\in(0,T)
; then for
t\in[0,\overline{t}]
,
F
is Lipschitz continuous with parameter

\sqrt{2\left(1+\frac{1}{(T-\overline{t})^{2}}\right)}\max\left\{1,L\right\}

in
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
.
By classical ODE theory, Lipschitz continuity of
F
on
[0,\overline{t}]
implies that the solution
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,\overline{t}],\mathbb{R}^{2d})
uniquely exists.
Since
\overline{t}\in(0,T)
is arbitrary, the solution uniquely exists on
[0,T)
and the proof is complete.
∎

Corollary H.2
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Lipschitz continuous and let
T>0
.
The solution
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
to (
88
) satisfies the differential equation

\displaystyle\ddot{X}(t)+\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)+\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))=0

(89)

almost everywhere in
t\in[0,T)
.
Conversely, if
X\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,T),\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies (
89
) almost everywhere in
t\in[0,T)
, then with
Z\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined as
Z(t)=-\dot{X}(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
,
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
is the solution of (
88
).

Proof.

Let
L
be the Lipschitz continuity parameter of
{\mathbb{A}}
and take
\overline{t}\in(0,T)
.
As
t\mapsto\dot{X}(t)
is continuous on the compact interval
[0,\overline{t}]
, we have
M=\max_{t\in[0,\overline{t}]}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|<\infty
.
Therefore
t\mapsto X(t)
is
M
-Lipschitz, which implies that
t\mapsto{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
is
LM
-Lipschitz; hence
{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
is differentiable almost everywhere in
t
.
Then
\dot{X}(t)=-Z(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
is absolutely continuous in
t
(being the sum of
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
function
Z
and Lipschitz continuous
{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))
) and hence differentiable almost everywhere.
Now the equivalence between (
88
) and (
89
) can be proved via same arguments as in
Section

B.6
, with all equalities holding almost everywhere in
t
.
∎

H.3.2
Regularity at the terminal time
t=T

Next, we show the solution on
[0,T)
can be continuously extended to the terminal time
t=T
due to its favorable properties.
First, we need:

Lemma H.3
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Lipschitz continuous and monotone.
Let
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
be the solution to (
88
) on
[0,T)
.
Then

\displaystyle\Psi(t)=\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}

is nonincreasing in
t\in[0,T)
.
In particular, this implies

\displaystyle\left\|\dot{Z}(t)\right\|^{2}=\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{T^{2}}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

Take the inner product with
\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\dot{X}(t)
to both sides of (
89
), we have for almost every
t\in[0,T)
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\langle\ddot{X}(t),\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle+\frac{1}{(T-t)^{3}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\langle\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)),\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{1}{2(T-t)^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\langle\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)),\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle.

Note that because
\dot{X}(t)
is absolutely continuous,
\Psi(t)
is also absolutely continuous.
Integrating from
0
to
t
and reorganizing, we obtain the following “conservation law”:

\displaystyle E\equiv\frac{1}{2T^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(0)\right\|^{2}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{2(T-t)^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}}_{\frac{\Psi(t)}{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{(T-s)^{2}}\left\langle\frac{d}{ds}{\mathbb{A}}(X(s)),\dot{X}(s)\right\rangle ds

(
E
is a constant).
Note that by monotonicity of
{\mathbb{A}}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)),\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}(X(t+h))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)),X(t+h)-X(t)\right\rangle}{h^{2}}\geq 0.

Therefore for almost every
t\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\dot{\Psi}(t)=-\frac{2}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\langle\frac{d}{dt}A(X(t)),\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle\leq 0,

from which we conclude that
\Psi(t)
is nonincreasing.
Finally, (
88
) gives
\dot{Z}(t)=\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)
and
\dot{X}(0)=-Z(0)-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})=-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})
, so we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\|\dot{Z}(t)\right\|^{2}=\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}=\Psi(t)\leq\Psi(0)=\frac{1}{T^{2}}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|^{2}.

∎

Corollary H.4
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Lipschitz continuous and monotone.
Then, given the solution
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
to (
88
) on
[0,T)
, one can continuously extend
X(t)
to
t=T
, and the extension
X\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\dot{X}(t)=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\frac{X(t)-X(T)}{t-T}=0.

Proof.

Let
t\in[0,T)
and let
h>0
satisfy
t+h<T
. Then by
Lemma

H.3
,

\displaystyle\left\|X(t+h)-X(t)\right\|=\left\|\int_{t}^{t+h}\dot{X}(s)ds\right\|\leq\int_{t}^{t+h}\left\|\dot{X}(s)\right\|ds\leq\int_{t}^{t+h}\frac{T-s}{T}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|ds\leq h\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|.

This shows that
X\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Lipschitz continuous, which implies any sequence
\{X(t_{j})\}
with
0<t_{j}\nearrow T
is a Cauchy sequence, and such sequential limit is unique.
Setting
X(T)
to this unique limit, we see that
X\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a (unique) continuous function extending
X
.
Additionally,
Lemma

H.3
gives

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|\leq\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\frac{T-t}{T}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|=0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\left\|\frac{X(t)-X(T)}{t-T}\right\|=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\frac{1}{T-t}\left\|\int_{t}^{T}\dot{X}(s)ds\right\|\leq\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\frac{1}{T-t}\int_{t}^{T}\left\|\dot{X}(s)\right\|ds=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|=0

where the second-to-last equality uses L’Hôpital’s rule.
∎

H.3.3
Proof of
Theorem

7.1

Recall that we define
V\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle V(t)

\displaystyle=-\left\|Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}+2\left\langle Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),\frac{1}{T-t}\left(X(t)-X(T)\right)\right\rangle.

Differentiate
V
and apply the substitutions
Z=-(\dot{X}+{\mathbb{A}}(X))
and
\dot{Z}=\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}
to obtain

\displaystyle\dot{V}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\left\langle\dot{Z}(t),Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\rangle+2\left\langle\dot{Z}(t),\frac{1}{T-t}\left(X(t)-X(T)\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+2\left\langle Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{1}{T-t}\left(X(t)-X(T)\right)\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=2\left\langle\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t),\dot{X}(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))+\frac{1}{T-t}\left(X(t)-X(T)\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad-2\left\langle\dot{X}(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left(X(t)-X(T)\right)+\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\langle X(t)-X(T),{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 0.

On the other hand, by
Corollary

H.4
, we have
\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\dot{X}(t)=0
, which implies

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}Z(t)=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\left(-\dot{X}(t)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))

and thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}V(t)=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\left(-\left\|Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}\right)+\lim_{t\to T^{-}}2\left\langle Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),\frac{X(t)-X(T)}{T-t}\right\rangle=0

where the last equality holds because both
Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))
and
\frac{X(t)-X(T)}{t-T}
converges to
0
as
t\to T^{-}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}V(t)\leq V(0)=-\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}-\frac{2}{T}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),X(T)-X_{0}\right\rangle

where the last equality uses
Z(0)=0
.
Now multiplying
-\frac{T}{2}
to both sides and applying
Lemma

3.2
, we conclude

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|X_{0}-X_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{T^{2}}.

H.3.4
Correspondence of continuous-time analysis with discrete-time analysis

We overview how the continuous-time analysis presented above corresponds to respective analyses of
Dual-OHM
and
Dual-FEG
.
Precisely, we verify that under the identifcation of terms from
Section

H.1
, the following holds true:

(i)

The discrete Lyapunov function
V_{k}
corresponds to
V(t)

(ii)

The consecutive difference
V_{k+1}-V_{k}
corresponds to
\dot{V}(t)

•

Dual-OHM

In
Section

H.1.1
we consider
Dual-OHM
with
{\mathbb{T}}=2{\mathbb{J}}_{\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}}-{\mathbb{I}}
for continuous monotone
{\mathbb{A}}
to derive the continuous-time limit, and we have the identities
x_{k+1}={\mathbb{J}}_{\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}}y_{k}
and
x_{k+1}+\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}=y_{k}
.
Therefore, the corresponding Lyapunov analysis of
Dual-OHM
should use
g_{k}=\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}
(instead of
g_{k}=\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{k}
).
Recall that we identify
\alpha k=t
,
\alpha N=T
,
x_{k}=X(t)
,
y_{k}=Y(t)
and
\frac{z_{k}}{\alpha}=Z(t)
.

(i)

\frac{V_{k}}{\alpha^{2}}\,\longleftrightarrow\,V(t)
.

Replacing
g_{N}=\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}x_{N}
with
\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}
in the Lyapunov function of
Dual-OHM
gives

\displaystyle V_{k}=-\frac{N-k-1}{N-k}\left\|z_{k}+\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle z_{k}+\alpha{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},y_{k}-y_{N-1}\right\rangle.

Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{V_{k}}{\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha(N-k)}\right)\left\|\frac{z_{k}}{\alpha}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{\alpha(N-k)}\left\langle\frac{z_{k}}{\alpha}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},y_{k}-y_{N-1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)\right)\left\|Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{T-t}\left\langle Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),Y(t)-Y(T-\alpha)\right\rangle.

From the identity
x_{k+1}+\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}=y_{k}
we have
Y(t)=X(t+\alpha)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)
, so in the limit
\alpha\to 0
,
Y(t)=X(t)
.
Then the above equation establishes the desired correspondence, as

(\textrm{RHS})=-\left\|Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{T-t}\left\langle Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),X(t)-X(T)\right\rangle.

(ii)

\frac{\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}V_{k+1}-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}V_{k}}{\alpha}\,\longleftrightarrow\,\dot{V}(t)
.

The Lyapunov analysis of
Dual-OHM
establishes

\displaystyle V_{k+1}-V_{k}=-\frac{4}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\langle x_{k+1}-x_{N},g_{k+1}-g_{N}\right\rangle.

Replacing
g_{k}
with
\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}
, dividing both sides by
\alpha^{3}
and applying the identifications, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}V_{k+1}-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}V_{k}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\alpha^{3}(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\langle x_{k+1}-x_{N},\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1}-\frac{\alpha}{2}{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{(\alpha N-\alpha k)(\alpha N-\alpha k-\alpha)}\left\langle x_{k+1}-x_{N},{\mathbb{A}}(X(t+\alpha)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{(T-t)^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)}\left\langle X(t+\alpha)-X(T),{\mathbb{A}}(X(t+\alpha)-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\rangle.

Taking
\alpha\to 0
, we have

\displaystyle(\textrm{RHS})=-\frac{2}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\langle X(t)-X(T),{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\rangle=\dot{V}(t)

which gives the desired correspondence.

•

Dual-FEG

As above, we use the identification
\alpha k=t
,
\alpha N=T
,
x_{k}=X(t)
and
z_{k}=Z(t)
.

(i)

\frac{V_{k}}{\alpha}\,\longleftrightarrow\,V(t)
.

The Lyapunov function of
Dual-FEG
is

\displaystyle V_{k}=-\alpha\left\|z_{k}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{N-k}\left\langle z_{k}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{k}-x_{N}\right\rangle.

Dividing both sides by
\alpha
and applying the identifcations, we have

\displaystyle\frac{V_{k}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\left\|z_{k}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{\alpha(N-k)}\left\langle z_{k}+{\mathbb{A}}x_{N},x_{k}-x_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left\|Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{T-t}\left\langle Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)),X(t)-X(T)\right\rangle,

we get the desired correspondence.

(ii)

\frac{\frac{1}{\alpha}V_{k+1}-\frac{1}{\alpha}V_{k}}{\alpha}\,\longleftrightarrow\,\dot{V}(t)
.

The Lyapunov analysis of
Dual-FEG
establishes

\displaystyle V_{k+1}-V_{k}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2},x_{N}-x_{k+1/2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\alpha(N-k)^{2}}\Big(\|x_{k+1/2}-x_{k}\|^{2}-\alpha^{2}\|{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\|^{2}\Big)

Because
x_{k+1/2}=x_{k}+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)
, dividing the both sides by
\alpha^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{\alpha}V_{k+1}-\frac{1}{\alpha}V_{k}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}(N-k)(N-k-1)}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}x_{N}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2},x_{N}-x_{k+1/2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}(N-k)^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha}\|x_{k+1/2}-x_{k}\|^{2}-\alpha\|{\mathbb{A}}x_{k+1/2}-{\mathbb{A}}x_{k}\|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{(T-t)^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)}\left\langle{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)),X(T)-X(t)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{-\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha}\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})-\alpha\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\|^{2}\Big)}_{{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)}.

Taking
\alpha\to 0
, we have

\displaystyle(\textrm{RHS})=-\frac{2}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\langle X(t)-X(T),{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\rangle=\dot{V}(t)

which gives the desired correspondence.

H.4
Generalization to differential inclusion with maximally monotone
{\mathbb{A}}

So far, we have analyzed the Dual-Anchor ODE with respect to Lipschitz continuous
{\mathbb{A}}
.
In this section, we deal with its extension to differential inclusion, which is a generalized form of continuous-time model, covering the case of general (possibly set-valued) maximally monotone operators:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}\dot{X}(t)\\
\dot{Z}(t)\end{pmatrix}&\in-\begin{pmatrix}Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\\
\frac{1}{T-t}Z(t)+\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

(90)

We say that
(X(t),Z(t))
is a solution to this differential inclusion if it satisfies (
90
) almost everywhere in
t
. (So unlike in ODEs, we do not require
X,Z
to be differentiable everywhere, but only require absolute continuity, which implies differentiability almost everywhere.)
Although (
90
) is technically not an ODE, with a slight abuse of notation, we will often refer to it as (generalized) Dual-Anchor ODE throughout the section.

H.4.1
Existence of a solution to the generalized Dual-Anchor ODE

Theorem H.5
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
be maximally monotone and let
T>0
.
Given the initial conditions
X(0)=X_{0}\in\mathrm{dom}\,{\mathbb{A}}
and
Z(0)=0
, there exists a solution to the generalized Dual-Anchor ODE, i.e., an absolutely continuous curve
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
that satisfies (
90
) for
t\in(0,T)
almost everywhere.

We proceed with similar ideas as in
(
Aubin & Cellina, 1984
, Chpater 3)
and
(
Suh et al., 2023
, Appendix B.2)
, i.e., we construct a sequence
\{(X_{\delta}(t),Z_{\delta}(t))\}_{\delta>0}
of solutions to ODEs approximating (
90
), with
{\mathbb{A}}
replaced by Yosida approximations
{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}
(which are much better-behaved, being single-valued and Lipschitz continuous).
Then we show that the limit of
(X_{\delta}(t),Z_{\delta}(t))
in
\delta\to 0
is a solution to the original inclusion (
90
).

Below we present some well-known facts needed to rigorously establish the approximation argument.

Lemma H.6
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
be maximally monotone.
Then for
\delta>0
, the Yosida approximation operators

{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}=\frac{1}{\delta}\left({\mathbb{I}}-{\mathbb{J}}_{\delta{\mathbb{A}}}\right)=\frac{1}{\delta}\left({\mathbb{I}}-\left({\mathbb{I}}+\delta{\mathbb{A}}\right)^{-1}\right)

satisfy the followings:

(i)

\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}(x)\in{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbb{J}}_{\delta{\mathbb{A}}}x)
.

(ii)

{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is singled-valued,
\frac{1}{\delta}
-Lipschitz continuous and maximally monotone.

(iii)

\left\|{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}(x)\right\|\leq\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(x))\right\|
.

(iv)

\lim_{\delta\to 0+}{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}(x)=m({\mathbb{A}}(x)):=\argmin_{u\in{\mathbb{A}}(x)}\|u\|
. (The minimum norm element of the set
{\mathbb{A}}(x)
is well-defined because
{\mathbb{A}}(x)
is a closed and convex set due to maximal monotonicity of
{\mathbb{A}}
.)

Proof.

See
(
Aubin & Cellina, 1984
, Chpater 3.1, Theorem 2)
.
∎

Lemma H.7
.

Let
\{x_{n}(\cdot)\}_{n}
be a sequence of absolutely continuous functions
x_{n}\colon I\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval of finite length.
Suppose that

(i)

\forall t\in I
, the set
\{x_{n}(t)\}_{n}
is bounded.

(ii)

There exists
M>0
such that
\left\|\dot{x}_{n}(t)\right\|\leq M
almost everywhere in
t\in I
.

Then there exists a subsequence
x_{n_{k}}(\cdot)
such that

(1)

x_{n_{k}}(\cdot)
uniformly converges to
x(\cdot)
over compact subsets of
I
.

(2)

x(\cdot)
is absolutely continuous, so
\dot{x}
exists almost everywhere.

(3)

\dot{x}_{n_{k}}(\cdot)
converges weakly to
\dot{x}(\cdot)
in
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

This is a simplified version of
(
Aubin & Cellina, 1984
, Chapter 0.3, Theorem 4)
. For completeness, we outline the proof below.

The family
\{x_{n}(\cdot)\}_{n}
is pointwisely bounded (condition (i)) and equicontinuous as

\left\|x_{n}(t)-x_{n}(s)\right\|=\left\|\int_{s}^{t}\dot{x}_{n}(\tau)\,d\tau\right\|\leq M|t-s|,\quad\forall s,t\in I.

By Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, there exists a subsequence
x_{n_{k}}(\cdot)
such that
x_{n_{k}}\to x
uniformly over compact subsets of
I
, where
x\colon I\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is continuous (so far we have not shown absolute continuity).
Now observe that
\{\dot{x}_{n_{k}}(\cdot)\}_{k}
is a bounded set within
L^{\infty}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})=L^{1}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})^{*}
.
Therefore, by Banach-Alaoglu theorem,
\dot{x}_{n_{k}}(\cdot)
again has a subsequence that converges in the weak-
*
sense.
By replacing
n_{k}
with this subsequence if necessary, assume without loss of generality that
\dot{x}_{n_{k}}\xrightarrow{\text{w}*}y\in L^{\infty}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
This means that for any
c\in L^{1}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{I}\left\langle\dot{x}_{n_{k}}(\tau),c(\tau)\right\rangle\,d\tau=\int_{I}\left\langle y(\tau),c(\tau)\right\rangle\,d\tau.

(91)

For any
s,t\in I
and
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, taking
c(\tau)=1_{[s,t]}(\tau){\mathbf{a}}
in particular, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{s}^{t}\left\langle\dot{x}_{n_{k}}(\tau),{\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle\,d\tau=\int_{s}^{t}\left\langle y(\tau),{\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle\,d\tau=\left\langle\int_{s}^{t}y(\tau)\,d\tau,{\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle.

On the other hand, the left hand side equals
\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\langle x_{n_{k}}(t)-x_{n_{k}}(s),{\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle=\left\langle x(t)-x(s),{\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle
.
Since
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is arbitrary, this shows

x(t)-x(s)=\int_{s}^{t}y(\tau)\,d\tau,\quad y\in L^{\infty}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})

so
x
is absolutely continuous (in fact, Lipschitz continuous) and
\dot{x}=y
almost everywhere.
Finally, note that
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{1}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})
(as
I
is of finite length), so we can take
c\in L^{2}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})
in (
91
), showing that
\dot{x}_{n_{k}}\to y
weakly in
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
∎

Using the above results, we can now prove
Theorem

H.5
.
For
\delta>0
, the ODE

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}\dot{X}_{\delta}(t)\\
\dot{Z}_{\delta}(t)\end{pmatrix}&=-\begin{pmatrix}Z_{\delta}(t)+{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}(X_{\delta}(t))\\
\frac{1}{T-t}Z_{\delta}(t)+\frac{1}{T-t}{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}(X_{\delta}(t))\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(92)

with initial conditions
X_{\delta}(0)=X_{0}\in\mathrm{dom}\,{\mathbb{A}}
and
Z_{\delta}(0)=0
has a unique
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
solution
(X_{\delta}(t),Z_{\delta}(t))
for
t\in[0,T]
by
Theorem

H.1
and
Corollary

H.4
.
Additionally by
Lemma

H.3
,
X_{\delta}
and
Z_{\delta}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta}(t)\right\|\leq\frac{T-t}{T}\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|\leq\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|,\qquad\left\|\dot{Z}_{\delta}(t)\right\|\leq\frac{1}{T}\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|.

(93)

Therefore, we can apply
Lemma

H.7
to obtain a positive sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
such that

•

\begin{pmatrix}X_{\delta_{n}}\\
Z_{\delta_{n}}\end{pmatrix}\to\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
uniformly on
[0,T]
, where
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
is absolutely continuous,

•

\begin{pmatrix}\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}\\
\dot{Z}_{\delta_{n}}\end{pmatrix}\to\begin{pmatrix}\dot{X}\\
\dot{Z}\end{pmatrix}
weakly in
L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

Now, we wish to show that
(X(t),Z(t))
is a solution to (
90
).
Define
G_{\delta},G\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

\displaystyle G_{\delta}(t)=Z_{\delta}(t)+\dot{X}_{\delta}(t),\quad G(t)=Z(t)+\dot{X}(t).

By construction,

\displaystyle G_{\delta_{n}}(\cdot)\to G(\cdot)\quad\textit{weakly }\,\text{in }\,L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Note that because
(X_{\delta},Z_{\delta})
solves (
92
) and
Lemma

H.6
(i) holds, we have

\displaystyle G_{\delta_{n}}(t)=-{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta}(X_{\delta}(t))\in-{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbb{J}}_{\delta_{n}{\mathbb{A}}}(X_{\delta_{n}}(t))).

On the other hand, because
Z_{\delta_{n}}(0)=0
, by (
93
) we have

\displaystyle\left\|Z_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|=\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\dot{Z}_{\delta_{n}}(s)ds\right\|\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\dot{Z}_{\delta_{n}}(s)\right\|ds\leq\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\left\|{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta_{n}}(X_{0})\right\|}{T}ds\leq\left\|{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta_{n}}(X_{0})\right\|\leq\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|.

Together with the norm bound on
\dot{X}
in (
93
), this implies that for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\|X_{\delta_{n}}(t)-{\mathbb{J}}_{\delta_{n}{\mathbb{A}}}(X_{\delta_{n}}(t))\right\|=\delta_{n}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}_{\delta_{n}}(X_{\delta_{n}}(t))\right\|=\delta_{n}\left\|Z_{\delta_{n}}(t)+\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|\leq\delta_{n}\left(\left\|Z_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|+\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\|\right)\leq 2\delta_{n}\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|.

As
X_{\delta_{n}}(\cdot)
converges uniformly to
X(\cdot)
, the above result shows that
{\mathbb{J}}_{\delta_{n}{\mathbb{A}}}(X_{\delta_{n}}(\cdot))
uniformly converges to
X(\cdot)
as well. In particular,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{J}}_{\delta_{n}{\mathbb{A}}}(X_{\delta_{n}}(\cdot))\to X(\cdot)\quad\textit{strongly }\,\text{in }\,L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Now, define
\mathcal{A}\colon L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

{\mathcal{A}}(y)=\left\{u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})\,|\,u(t)\in{\mathbb{A}}(y(t))\,\,\text{for a.e.}\,\,t\in[0,T]\right\}.

{\mathcal{A}}
is monotone because
{\mathbb{A}}
is monotone: if
u\in{\mathcal{A}}(y),v\in{\mathcal{A}}(z)
then

\left\langle u-v,y-z\right\rangle_{L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle u(t)-v(t),y(t)-z(t)\right\rangle\,dt\geq 0\quad\text{since}\,\,u(t)\in{\mathbb{A}}(y(t)),v(t)\in{\mathbb{A}}(z(t))\,\,\text{a.e. in}\,\,t\in[0,T].

If
{\mathcal{I}}\colon L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the identity operator, then
{\mathcal{I}}+{\mathcal{A}}
is surjective: for any
u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have
y(t)={\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}(u(t))\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
because
{\mathbb{J}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}
is nonexpansive, and then
u\in{\mathcal{A}}(y)
by construction.
By Minty’s surjectivity theorem
(
Minty, 1962
)
, this implies that
{\mathcal{A}}
is maximally monotone.
Now because
{\mathbb{J}}_{\delta_{n}{\mathbb{A}}}(X_{\delta_{n}})
converges to
X
strongly and
-G_{\delta_{n}}\in{\mathcal{A}}({\mathbb{J}}_{\delta_{n}{\mathbb{A}}}(X_{\delta_{n}}))
converges to
-G
weakly, and the graph of a maximally monotone operator is closed under the strong-weak topology
(
Bauschke & Combettes, 2017
, Proposition 20.38)
, we conclude that
-G\in{\mathcal{A}}(X)
, i.e.,

G(t)=Z(t)+\dot{X}(t)\in-{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\iff\dot{X}(t)\in-\left(Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right)\quad\text{a.e. in}\,\,t\in[0,T].

Finally, because
(T-t)\dot{Z}_{\delta}(t)=\dot{X}_{\delta}(t)
for all
\delta>0
we have

(T-t)\dot{Z}_{\delta_{n}}=\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}\xrightarrow{\text{w}}\dot{X}\quad\text{in}\,\,L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}).

On the other hand, we had
\dot{Z}_{\delta_{n}}\xrightarrow{\text{w}}\dot{Z}
in
L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which implies

(T-t)\dot{Z}_{\delta_{n}}\xrightarrow{\text{w}}(T-t)\dot{Z}

because for any
c(t)\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have
(T-t)c(t)\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
as well.
By uniqueness of the (weak) limit, we have
\dot{X}=(T-t)\dot{Z}
a.e., so

\dot{Z}(t)=\frac{1}{T-t}\dot{X}(t)\in-\frac{1}{T-t}\left(Z(t)+{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right)\quad\text{a.e. in}\,\,t\in[0,T].

This shows that
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
is indeed a solution of (
90
).

H.4.2
Behavior at the terminal time
t=T

So far, we have successfully constructed an absolutely continuous solution
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
In this section we show that
X(t),Z(t)
have two very favorable properties at the terminal time:
X
has left derivative 0, and
Z(T)\in-{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))
.
To show this, we need:

Lemma H.8
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
be maximally monotone and let
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
be the solution of (
90
) constructed in
Theorem

H.5
.
Then for almost every
t\in[0,T)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{T^{2}}\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
\delta_{n}>0
be the sequence taken in the proof of
Theorem

H.5
, for which the solutions
(X_{\delta_{n}},Z_{\delta_{n}})
to (
92
) with
\delta=\delta_{n}
converge uniformly to
(X,Z)
and
(\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}},\dot{Z}_{\delta_{n}})
converge weakly to
(\dot{X},\dot{Z})
.
We have shown
\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|\leq\frac{T-t}{T}\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|
in
Equation

93
.
Thus, the proof is done once we show

\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|

for almost every
t\in[0,T]
.
(This would be straightforward if
\dot{X}
were the pointwise limit of
\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}
, but it is not the case; it is the weak limit. We need a careful argument, as presented below.)

Let
D
be any measurable subset of
[0,T]
.
Since
\dot{X}_{\delta_{n_{k}}}\to\dot{X}
weakly in
L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\chi_{D}\dot{X}\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{D}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}dt&=\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\dot{X}(t),\chi_{D}(t)\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle dt=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t),\chi_{D}(t)\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle dt\\
&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{D}\left\langle\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t),\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle dt\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{D}\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|dt.\end{aligned}

(94)

Now from
\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(\cdot)\right\|\leq\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|
and
\left\|\dot{X}(\cdot)\right\|\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{1}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(\cdot)\right\|\left\|\dot{X}(\cdot)\right\|\leq\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|\left\|\dot{X}(\cdot)\right\|\in L^{1}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Thus by reverse Fatou Lemma,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{D}\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|dt\leq\int_{D}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|dt.

Combining the above inequality with (
94
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{D}\left(\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|-\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|\right)\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|dt\geq 0.

As
D
was an arbitrary measurable subset of
[0,T]
, we conclude that for almost every
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left(\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|-\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|\right)\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|\geq 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\dot{X}_{\delta_{n}}(t)\right\|.

∎

Corollary H.9
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
be maximally monotone and let
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
be the solution of (
90
) constructed in
Theorem

H.5
.
Then the following holds true:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\frac{X(t)-X(T)}{t-T}=0,\qquad-Z(T)\in{\mathbb{A}}(X(T)).

Proof.

Using
Lemma

H.8
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\left\|\frac{X(t)-X(T)}{t-T}\right\|\leq\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\int_{t}^{T}\frac{\left\|\dot{X}(s)\right\|}{T-t}ds\leq\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\int_{t}^{T}\frac{\left\|\dot{X}(s)\right\|}{T-s}ds\leq\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\int_{t}^{T}\frac{\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|}{T}ds=0,

which proves the first property.

Next, because
(X(t),Z(t))
satisfies (
90
) a.e. and
\|\dot{X}(t)\|\leq\frac{T-t}{T}\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X_{0}))\right\|
a.e. (by
Lemma

H.8
), we can take a sequence
t_{k}\in(0,T)
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k}=T
and

\displaystyle-\left(\dot{X}(t_{k})+Z(t_{k})\right)\in{\mathbb{A}}(X(t_{k})),\qquad\left\|\dot{X}(t_{k})\right\|\leq\frac{T-t_{k}}{T}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|.

Then we have
\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\|\dot{X}(t_{k})\right\|\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{T-t_{k}}{T}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|=0
.
On the other hand,
X(t_{k})\to X(T)
and
Z(t_{k})\to Z(T)
because
X,Z
are continuous.
Finally, because the graph of
{\mathbb{A}}
is closed in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

(
Bauschke & Combettes, 2017
, Proposition 20.38)
, we conclude

\displaystyle-Z(T)=-\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(\dot{X}(t_{k})+Z(t_{k})\right)\in{\mathbb{A}}\left(\lim_{k\to\infty}X(t_{k})\right)={\mathbb{A}}(X(T)).

∎

H.4.3
Convergence analysis

Based on the previous analyses, we can prove that the constructed solution has a Lyapunov function similar to that of
Theorem

7.1
(the case of Lipschitz continuous operators).

Theorem H.10
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{d}
be maximally monotone and
\mathrm{Zer}\,{\mathbb{A}}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Z\end{pmatrix}
be the solution of (
90
) constructed in
Theorem

H.5
.
Then the function
V\colon[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle V(t)

\displaystyle=-\left\|Z(t)-Z(T)\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{T-t}\left\langle Z(t)-Z(T),X(t)-X(T)\right\rangle

is nonincreasing, and
\lim_{t\to T^{-}}V(t)=0
.
Furthermore, for
X_{\star}\in\mathrm{Zer}\,{\mathbb{A}}
,

\displaystyle\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X(T)))\right\|^{2}\leq\left\|Z(T)\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|X_{0}-X_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{T^{2}}

where
m({\mathbb{A}}(X(T)))
is the minimum norm element of
{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))
.

Proof.

Let
\delta_{n}>0
be the sequence taken in the proof of
Theorem

H.5
. Let

\displaystyle V_{\delta_{n}}(t)=-\left\|Z_{\delta_{n}}(t)-Z_{\delta_{n}}(T)\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{T-t}\left\langle Z_{\delta_{n}}(t)-Z_{\delta_{n}}(T),X_{\delta_{n}}(t)-X_{\delta_{n}}(T)\right\rangle

for
t\in[0,T)
.
As
\begin{pmatrix}X_{\delta_{n}}\\
Z_{\delta_{n}}\end{pmatrix}
converges uniformly on
[0,T]
, for all
t\in[0,T)
we have

\displaystyle V(t)=\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{\delta_{n}}(t).

Observe that
V_{\delta_{n}}(\cdot)
is nonincreasing on
[0,T)
for each
n
(by the result of
Theorem

7.1
and
Z_{\delta_{n}}(T)=-{\mathbb{A}}(X_{\delta_{n}}(T))
, which follows from
Corollary

H.9
and uniqueness of the solution in the Lipschitz case).
Therefore, for any
s,t\in[0,T)
such that
s<t
, we have

\displaystyle V(s)=\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{\delta_{n}}(s)\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{\delta_{n}}(t)=V(t),

which shows that
V
is nonincreasing.
Furthermore, from
\lim_{t\to T^{-}}Z(t)=Z(T)
(continuity of
Z
) and
Corollary

H.9
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}V(t)=-\left\|\lim_{t\to T^{-}}Z(t)-Z(T)\right\|^{2}-2\left\langle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}Z(t)-Z(T),\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\frac{X(t)-X(T)}{t-T}\right\rangle=0.

Therefore

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{t\to T^{-}}V(t)\leq V(0)=-\left\|Z(T)\right\|^{2}-\frac{2}{T}\left\langle Z(T),X_{0}-X(T)\right\rangle,

and
Lemma

3.2
gives

\displaystyle\left\|Z(T)\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\left\|X_{0}-X_{\star}\right\|^{2}}{T^{2}}.

Finally, because
-Z(T)\in{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))
by
Corollary

H.9
, the left hand side is lower bounded by
\left\|m({\mathbb{A}}(X(T)))\right\|^{2}
, which gives the desired convergence rate.

∎

Appendix I
Omitted details from
Section

8

I.1
Extragradient algorithm specification

In Figures
1(a)
,
2(a)
, we display comparison with Extragradient (EG)
(
Korpelevich, 1976
)
.
For completeness, we specify its definition here:

\displaystyle x_{k+1/2}

\displaystyle=x_{k}-\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k})

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=x_{k}-\alpha\nabla_{\pm}\mathbf{L}(x_{k+1/2}).

I.2
Details of worst-case construction from
Ouyang & Xu (2021)

For
Figure

2(a)
, we use the following construction due to
Ouyang & Xu (2021)
, which has been used to establish complexity lower bounds:

A=\frac{1}{4}\begin{bmatrix}&&&-1&1\\
&&\iddots&\iddots&\\
&-1&1\\
-1&1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\quad b=\frac{1}{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\vdots\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad g=\frac{1}{4}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

and
G=2A^{\intercal}A
.
In
Ouyang & Xu (2021)
, it is shown that
\|A\|\leq\frac{1}{2}
(so
\|G\|\leq\frac{1}{2}
), so that the bilinear function

{\mathbf{L}}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}u^{\intercal}Gu-g^{\intercal}u-\left\langle Au-b,v\right\rangle

is
1
-smooth.

I.3
H-dual algorithms produce identical terminal iterates for linear operators

In this section, we only consider explicit algorithms written in the form

\displaystyle x_{k+1}=x_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}h_{k+1,j}{\mathbb{A}}x_{j}

(95)

where
{\mathbb{A}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a single-valued operator.
If
{\mathbb{A}}=\nabla_{\pm}{\mathbf{L}}
,
FEG
and
Dual-FEG
are instances of this class if we identify
1/2
-indexed iterates with integer iterates in increasing order.
If
{\mathbb{A}}=\nabla f
, constant step-size gradient descent or Nesterov’s accelerated gradient method
(
Nesterov, 1983
)
are instances of this class.
However, we do not need to restrict the discussion of this section to a specific operator class, because the following result does not rely on any particular property of the operator except for linearity.

Proposition I.1
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{A}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is linear, i.e., there exists a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that
{\mathbb{A}}x=Ax
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
N\geq 1
and consider an algorithm defined by (
95
) for
k=0,1,\dots,N-1
.
Then its H-dual algorithm, defined by

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{k+1}=\hat{x}_{k}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}h_{N-j,N-k-1}{\mathbb{A}}x_{j},

satisfies

\displaystyle x_{N}=\hat{x}_{N}

provided that
x_{0}=\hat{x}_{0}
.

Proof.

Because
{\mathbb{A}}
is linear, we can explicitly describe
x_{k}
from the algorithm (
95
) via matrix polynomial in
A
and
x_{0}
.
For
k=1,2,3
, we have

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=x_{0}-h_{1,0}Ax_{0}

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=x_{1}-h_{2,0}Ax_{0}-h_{2,1}Ax_{1}

\displaystyle=(x_{0}-h_{1,0}Ax_{0})-h_{2,0}Ax_{0}-h_{2,1}A(x_{0}-h_{1,0}Ax_{0})

\displaystyle=x_{0}-(h_{1,0}+h_{2,0}+h_{2,1})Ax_{0}+h_{1,0}h_{2,1}A^{2}x_{0}

and

\displaystyle x_{3}

\displaystyle=x_{2}-h_{3,0}Ax_{0}-h_{3,1}Ax_{1}-h_{3,2}Ax_{2}

\displaystyle=(x_{0}-(h_{1,0}+h_{2,0}+h_{2,1})Ax_{0}+h_{1,0}h_{2,1}A^{2}x_{0})-h_{3,0}Ax_{0}-h_{3,1}A(x_{0}-h_{1,0}Ax_{0})

\displaystyle\quad\quad-h_{3,2}A(x_{0}-(h_{1,0}+h_{2,0}+h_{2,1})Ax_{0}+h_{1,0}h_{2,1}A^{2}x_{0})

\displaystyle=x_{0}-(h_{1,0}+h_{2,0}+h_{3,0}+h_{2,1}+h_{3,1}+h_{3,2})Ax_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(h_{1,0}h_{2,1}+h_{1,0}h_{3,1}+h_{1,0}h_{3,2}+h_{2,0}h_{3,2}+h_{2,1}h_{3,2})A^{2}x_{0}-h_{1,0}h_{2,1}h_{3,2}A^{3}x_{0}.

The following lemma formally generalizes the pattern for these expressions:

Lemma I.2
.

For linear operator
{\mathbb{A}}x=Ax
, the iterates of (
95
) satisfy

\displaystyle x_{k}=\sum_{m=0}^{k}(-1)^{m}P(k,m)A^{m}x_{0}.

(96)

for
k=1,\dots,N
, where
P(k,0)=1
and for
m=1,\dots,k
,

\displaystyle P(k,m)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)\\
i(m)\leq k\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}h_{i(\ell),j(\ell)}.

(97)

Proof of
Lemma

I.2
.

We first clarify the definition of
P(k,m)
by providing some examples.
First, we have

\displaystyle P(1,1)=h_{1,0},\qquad P(2,1)=h_{1,0}+h_{2,0}+h_{2,1},\qquad P(3,1)=h_{1,0}+h_{2,0}+h_{3,0}+h_{2,1}+h_{3,1}+h_{3,2}.

This is because in (
97
), when
m=1
, the constraints
i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)
become vacuous so we add all
h_{i,j}
’s with
i\leq k
(note that always
j\leq i-1
).
Next, we have

\displaystyle P(2,2)=h_{1,0}h_{2,1},\qquad P(3,2)=h_{1,0}h_{2,1}+h_{1,0}h_{3,1}+h_{1,0}h_{3,2}+h_{2,0}h_{3,2}+h_{2,1}h_{3,2}.

Observe that by definition of
P
, we have to choose the
(i,j)
pairs within the product to satisfy the constraint
i(1)\leq j(2)
, so if we choose
(i(1),j(1))=(1,0)
then we must have
j(2)\geq 1
, so
P(k,1)
contains the products of
h_{1,0}
with
h_{i,j}
’s satisfying
k\geq i>j\geq 1
.
Similarly, if we choose
i(1)=2
then one must have
j(2)\geq 2
, and the only
(i,j)
pair satisfying
3\geq i>j\geq 2
is
(i,j)=(3,2)
, so
h_{2,0}h_{3,2}
and
h_{2,1}h_{3,2}
are the only terms within
P(3,2)
that involve
h_{2,0}
and
h_{2,1}
, respectively.

Because the constraint
j(\ell)<i(\ell)
is implicit, the definition (
97
) is requiring the
(i(\ell),j(\ell))
pairs consisting the product to satisfy

\displaystyle 1\leq i(1)\leq j(2)<\cdots<j(m-1)<i(m-1)\leq j(m)<i(m)\leq k.

Thus, when
m=k
, the only possible choice of indices is
i(\ell)=\ell
and
j(\ell)=\ell-1
, so

\displaystyle P(k,k)=h_{1,0}h_{2,1}\cdots h_{k,k-1}

and in particular,
P(3,3)=h_{1,0}h_{2,1}h_{3,2}
.

The above observations already show that (
96
) holds for
k=1,2,3
.
To prove the general statement, we use induction.
Suppose (
96
) holds up to
k
, and consider

\displaystyle x_{k+1}=x_{k}-\sum_{n=0}^{k}h_{k+1,n}Ax_{n}.

By induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{k}(-1)^{m}P(k,m)A^{m}x_{0}-\sum_{n=0}^{k}h_{k+1,n}A\left(\sum_{m=0}^{n}(-1)^{m}P(n,m)A^{m}x_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{k}(-1)^{m}P(k,m)A^{m}x_{0}+\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{n}(-1)^{m+1}h_{k+1,n}P(n,m)A^{m+1}x_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{k}(-1)^{m}P(k,m)A^{m}x_{0}+\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{m=1}^{n+1}(-1)^{m}h_{k+1,n}P(n,m-1)A^{m}x_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{k}(-1)^{m}P(k,m)A^{m}x_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{k+1}\sum_{n=m-1}^{k}(-1)^{m}h_{k+1,n}P(n,m-1)A^{m}x_{0}

\displaystyle=x_{0}+h_{k+1,k}P(k,k)A^{k+1}x_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{k}(-1)^{m}\left(P(k,m)+\sum_{n=m-1}^{k}h_{k+1,n}P(n,m-1)\right)A^{m}x_{0}.

Observe that
h_{k+1,k}P(k,k)=h_{k+1,k}\left(h_{1,0}h_{2,1}\cdots h_{k,k-1}\right)=h_{1,0}h_{2,1}\cdots h_{k+1,k}=P(k+1,k+1)
.
It remains to show that

\displaystyle P(k,m)+\sum_{n=m-1}^{k}h_{k+1,n}P(n,m-1)=P(k+1,m)

for
m=1,\dots,k
.
Rewrite the right hand side:

\displaystyle P(k+1,m)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)\\
i(m)\leq k+1\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}h_{i(\ell),j(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)\\
i(m)\leq k\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}h_{i(\ell),j(\ell)}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)\\
i(m)=k+1\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}h_{i(\ell),j(\ell)}

\displaystyle=P(k,m)+\sum_{n=1}^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)=n\end{subarray}}h_{k+1,n}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m-1}h_{i(\ell),j(\ell)}

\displaystyle=P(k,m)+\sum_{n=1}^{k}h_{k+1,n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)=n\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m-1}h_{i(\ell),j(\ell)}

\displaystyle=P(k,m)+\sum_{n=m-1}^{k}h_{k+1,n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-2)\leq j(m-1)\\
i(m)\leq n\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m-1}h_{i(\ell),j(\ell)}

(98)

where the last equality holds because for
n<m-1
, the inner summation with respect to
i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-2)\leq j(m-1)
is vacuous as it is impossible to choose
i(\ell)
’s satisfying
1\leq i(1)<i(2)<\cdots i(m-1)\leq n
.
Now replace the inner summation in (
98
) using the definition of
P(n,m-1)
:

\displaystyle P(k+1,m)=\sum_{n=m-1}^{k}h_{k+1,n}P(n,m-1).

This completes the induction.
∎

Lemma

I.2
explicitly characterizes
x_{k}
in terms of matrix polynomial.
Now the proof is done once we show that the explicit expression for
x_{N}
is invariant under anti-diagonal transposing, i.e., the replacement
h_{k,j}\mapsto h_{N-j,N-k}
.
More precisely, we have to show that
P(N,m)=\hat{P}(N,m)
, where

\displaystyle\hat{P}(N,m)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)\\
i(m)\leq N\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}h_{N-j(\ell),N-i(\ell)}

(99)

so that

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{N}=\sum_{m=0}^{N}(-1)^{m}\hat{P}(N,m)A^{m}\hat{x}_{0}.

Once this is done, provided that
\hat{x}_{0}=x_{0}
, we can conclude

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{N}=\sum_{m=0}^{N}(-1)^{m}\hat{P}(N,m)A^{m}\hat{x}_{0}=\sum_{m=0}^{N}(-1)^{m}P(N,m)A^{m}x_{0}=x_{N}.

In the product within (
99
), we make the substitution
i^{\prime}(\ell)=N-j(\ell)
and
j^{\prime}(\ell)=N-i(\ell)
.
Then for
\ell=1,\dots,m-1
,

\displaystyle i(\ell)\leq j(\ell+1)\iff N-j(\ell+1)\leq N-i(\ell)\iff i^{\prime}(\ell+1)\leq j^{\prime}(\ell)

and the condition
i(m)\leq N
is vacuous (can be dropped) because we are considering
h_{i,j}
only for
i=1,\dots,N
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\hat{P}(N,m)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i^{\prime}(m)\leq j^{\prime}(m-1),\dots,i^{\prime}(2)\leq j^{\prime}(1)\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}h_{i^{\prime}(\ell),j^{\prime}(\ell)}.

Finally, reversing the order of
(i^{\prime}(1),j^{\prime}(1)),\dots,(i^{\prime}(m),j^{\prime}(m))
via substitution
i(\ell)=i^{\prime}(m+1-\ell),j(\ell)=j^{\prime}(m+1-\ell)
we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{P}(N,m)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(1)\leq j(2),\dots,i(m-1)\leq j(m)\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}h_{i(\ell),j(\ell)}=P(N,m)

which concludes the proof.

∎

I.4
Faster convergence of the Dual-Anchor ODE with strongly monotone operators

In this section, we show that the Dual-Anchor ODE converges much more rapidly than the primal Anchor ODE for the subclass of strongly monotone operators, which suggests a new potential value of the dual algorithms.
While the Dual-Anchor ODE can only guarantee
\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
at the terminal time
T=\Omega(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
when
{\mathbb{A}}
is merely monotone, when
{\mathbb{A}}
is strongly monotone and Lipschitz, the Dual-Anchor ODE achieves
\|X(t)-X(T)\|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
and
\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
at
t={\mathcal{O}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})\ll T
and makes negligible progress thereafter (similar to the pattern shown in
Figure

2(b)
).
This allows one to apply
early stopping
; instead of waiting until the terminal time, we stop and return
X(t)
.
On the other hand, this is not the case for primal Anchor ODE which, even when
{\mathbb{A}}
is strongly monotone, behaves conservatively and converges no faster than the
\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\|^{2}={\mathcal{O}}(1/t^{2})
rate.
Below we provide the details.

Fast decay of
\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right\|
and
\left\|X(t)-X(T)\right\|
under strong monotonicity.

We established in
Lemma

H.3
that
\Psi(t)=\frac{1}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}
is nonincreasing by deriving

\displaystyle\dot{\Psi}(t)=-\frac{2}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\langle\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbb{A}}(X(t)),\dot{X}(t)\right\rangle.

If
{\mathbb{A}}
is
\mu
-strongly monotone for some
\mu>0
, then the above identity implies

\displaystyle\dot{\Psi}(t)\leq-\frac{2\mu}{(T-t)^{2}}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|^{2}=-2\mu\Psi(t)

so by Grönwall’s inequality,

\displaystyle\Psi(t)\leq\Psi(0)e^{-2\mu t}=\frac{\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|^{2}}{T^{2}}e^{-2\mu t}

for any
t\in(0,T)
.
Reorganizing, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|=\sqrt{(T-t)^{2}\Psi(t)}\leq\frac{T-t}{T}e^{-\mu t}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|\leq e^{-\mu t}\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X_{0})\right\|.

(100)

Complexity analysis.

Suppose the desired accuracy level is
\|{\mathbb{A}}(\cdot)\|\leq\epsilon
, so that we choose
T=\Theta(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
according to the worst-case guarantee
\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|={\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{T}\right)
.
Given that
{\mathbb{A}}
is
\mu
-strongly monotone and
L
-Lipschitz, one achieves

\displaystyle\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|={\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{LT^{2}}\right)\quad\text{at}\,\,t=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\mu}\log LT\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\mu}\log\frac{L}{\epsilon}\right)

by (
100
) and then

\displaystyle\left\|X(t)-X(T)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{T}\left\|\dot{X}(s)\right\|\,ds\leq\int_{t}^{T}\frac{T-s}{T-t}\left\|\dot{X}(t)\right\|\,ds\leq T{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{LT^{2}}\right)={\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{LT}\right)={\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{L}\right),

where the second inequality uses
\Psi(s)\leq\Psi(t)
for
s\geq t
.
Finally, this implies

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(t))\right\|\leq\left\|{\mathbb{A}}(X(T))\right\|+{\mathcal{O}}\left(L\left\|X(t)-X(T)\right\|\right)={\mathcal{O}}\left(\epsilon\right).

Anchor ODE is no faster than
{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)
under strong monotonicity.

Suppose
{\mathbb{A}}
is linear (
{\mathbb{A}}x=Ax
for some matrix
A
) and assume
A
is invertible. In this case the solution to the Anchor ODE can be explicitly characterized
(
Suh et al., 2023
)
:

\displaystyle X_{\text{anchor}}(t)=\frac{1}{t}A^{-1}\left(I-e^{-tA}\right)X_{0}.

Now if
A
is
\mu
-strongly monotone, then
A-\mu I
is monotone and
0\in\mathrm{Zer}\,(A-\mu I)
, so we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\|e^{-t(A-\mu I)}X_{0}\right\|^{2}=-2\left\langle(A-\mu I)e^{-t(A-\mu I)}X_{0},e^{-t(A-\mu I)}X_{0}\right\rangle\leq 0

\displaystyle\implies\left\|X_{0}\right\|\geq\left\|e^{-t(A-\mu I)}X_{0}\right\|=\left\|e^{\mu tI}e^{-tA}X_{0}\right\|=e^{\mu t}\left\|e^{-tA}X_{0}\right\|,

which implies

\displaystyle\left\|AX_{\text{anchor}}(t)\right\|=\frac{1}{t}\left\|\left(I-e^{-tA}\right)X_{0}\right\|\geq\frac{1}{t}(1-e^{-\mu t})\left\|X_{0}\right\|.

Therefore, the convergence of the Anchor ODE is generally not faster than
{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)
even with strongly monotone (and linear) operators.
